# Juicy: market research and startup validation report

Prepared 1 October 2026 for the founder of Juicy (the project called "Food Pickup" in this repository). Written for someone who is not from a business background: every business word is explained the first time it is used, and there is a glossary right after this introduction.

## Read this first

**What this is.** An honest check of whether Juicy solves a problem people will pay to fix, before more months go into building it. It was done the way an investor's team would do due diligence: start from what the code actually does, assume nothing is a good idea until the evidence says so, and look as hard for reasons it fails as for reasons it works.

**How it was done.** Three passes, as you asked.

1. Pass 1 (Part B): the full research study, split into five research workstreams run in parallel: the problem and customers; the restaurant side and pricing; competitors; market size and rules; and comparable startups and costs. India first, Bengaluru especially, with 2025 and 2026 sources.
2. Pass 2 (Part C): a separate research pass that saw only the neutral product fact sheet, never Pass 1, and was asked one question: "Why might this startup fail?"
3. Pass 3 (Part D): the two reports compared, specifically on restaurant willingness to pay, customer switching, competitors, market size, unit economics and whether Juicy is really different.

**How to read the labels.** Throughout the report a claim is one of four things. A **fact** is cited to a source you can click. An **estimate** is arithmetic on cited inputs, with the sums shown. An **assumption** is something the writer had to assume and says so. An **opinion** is a judgement. When reliable data does not exist, the report says so instead of inventing a number; there are several places where it does.

**Two limits you should know.** Web searches were rate-limited partway through each pass, so some claims rest on reading primary pages directly rather than on broad searches, and some things could not be verified at all; the report says so where it matters. And no field work was done: nobody stood in a canteen queue with a stopwatch or spoke to an owner. Sections 17 and 18 and Part E are about doing exactly that, because it is the evidence this report could not produce.

**The one-paragraph answer.** The queue problem Juicy targets is real but small, episodic and, remarkably, never measured in India. Where it has been solved (Bengaluru's own HungerBox and SmartQ), it was sold to the institution, not the café, and the orders earn about ₹5 to 6 each. Restaurants are angry at Zomato and Swiggy, but about delivery, not pickup, and pickup is already free or nearly free from several providers; the planned 10% commission is the weakest part of the plan, a conclusion both research passes reached separately. Customers will use order-ahead when a canteen makes it the default and not otherwise, and Swiggy's budget app Toing has just taught students to expect app prices at or below the counter. The realistic business is a campus-by-campus, sales-led utility sold to institutions and owner-run cafés at a flat or hybrid price, worth a few crore a year in Bengaluru, with a chance of becoming something larger only through institution contracts and outside capital. The next 30 days should be spent measuring the queue, running one outlet by hand, getting the payment and GST rules answered in writing, and asking for money, not building the platform in the roadmap.

## Founder decisions (7 October 2026)

After reading the first version of this report, you settled three points of the plan. They are fixed, and the rest of the report has been adjusted to match. Where an earlier suggestion conflicted with them, it is now marked as ruled out.

| Decision                                                                     | What it means in practice                                                                                                                                                                                                                  | How it fits the evidence                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| 1. **Web app first.** The website always exists; a store app may come later. | A student scans a QR code or opens a link and orders. Nothing to download. An Android or iPhone app is optional, later.                                                                                                                    | Fits well. No install barrier on cheap phones or metered data, and half of downloaded apps are deleted within a month anyway. SmartQ already runs a web app for exactly this job. The one cost: "your order is ready" alerts are less reliable on iPhones, which are about 6% of Indian phones. Build a store app only if a pilot shows a measured need, for example alerts not reaching people. |
| 2. **Never a rupee more for customers.**                                     | The price on Juicy is exactly the counter price: no platform fee, no convenience fee, no packaging charge, no queue-skip fee.                                                                                                              | Fits well, and removes the biggest switching risk this report found: Swiggy's Toing has taught students that app prices should match or beat the counter. It also means every rupee Juicy earns must come from the outlet or the institution, never the student.                                                                                                                                 |
| 3. **Only places with repeat, habituated customers.**                        | College canteens, crowded counters on campus, and the cafés, tea stalls, bakeries, juice and snack shops near colleges that the same students visit almost every day. Not offices, malls, families, cloud kitchens or general restaurants. | Fits the strongest evidence. Order-ahead survives only where there is a daily routine: people in captive cafeterias order through HungerBox about 7.5 times a month, while Ritual collapsed when its users' daily routine broke. Places near a college are also usually owner-run and set their own prices, so they can afford a small fee, and no HungerBox or SmartQ contract covers them.     |

### What these decisions change in the plan

- **Pricing.** Every option that charged the student is ruled out: the customer subscription, the per-order customer fee, the queue-skip fee, and the student's share of the hybrid (section 5, models D and E; section 9.5). What remains: a flat monthly fee from the outlet, a small per-order fee from the outlet, a campus fee from the college, or a mix of these.
- **GST is the one thing that could break the promise.** If a chartered accountant says Juicy, as an e-commerce operator, must charge 5% GST on orders from a canteen that charges none at its counter, then your promise means Juicy or the outlet pays it, not the student. On a ₹100 order that is ₹5, more than Juicy would earn from a 3% fee. Get that answer in writing before any real-money order (section 18, experiment 9).
- **Payments.** The payment-gateway fee (about 2% plus GST) cannot go on the student's bill either; NPCI already forbids passing UPI charges to customers. That favours the pilot design in this report: the outlet collects payment on its own UPI QR, so there is no gateway fee at all.
- **Where to sell.** The target list is now: (a) canteens and counters inside colleges, sold to the owner or contractor, or to the college itself; (b) owner-run cafés, tea stalls, bakeries, juice and snack shops within walking distance of a college gate. Offices and tech parks are dropped (section 11.1 is updated).
- **How to qualify a place.** Sign a place only if three things are true. The owner says most peak-hour customers are regulars, the same faces most days. The peak queue is long enough to measure: 8 minutes or more. The kitchen can prepare ahead.
- **The roadmap.** City-wide "store discovery by area" matters less. What matters is one list of every outlet in and around one college, so a student sees all the nearby counters in one place. Multi-store support is still needed, but per campus, not per city.

## Plain-words glossary

Every business word used in this report, explained once. Come back here whenever a term is unclear.

| Word                                 | What it means in plain words                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| ------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Aggregator                           | An app that lists many restaurants and takes orders for them. In India that means Zomato and Swiggy.                                                                                                                                                                   |
| Order ahead / pickup / takeaway      | You order and pay on your phone before you arrive, then collect the food yourself. No delivery rider.                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Commission (also "take rate")        | The slice of each order the platform keeps. A 10% commission on a ₹200 order is ₹20.                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| GMV (gross merchandise value)        | The total value of all orders that pass through the platform. It is NOT the platform's income; the commission is.                                                                                                                                                      |
| Revenue                              | The money the company actually earns (for Juicy: the commissions or fees), not the value of the food.                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Margin                               | What is left after a cost. "Gross margin" is revenue minus the direct cost of serving each order (payment fees, hosting). "Contribution margin" also subtracts the cost of getting and supporting the customer. "Net margin" subtracts everything, including salaries. |
| Unit economics                       | Whether you make or lose money on one single order or one single restaurant, before counting head-office costs.                                                                                                                                                        |
| SaaS (software as a service)         | Software you rent for a monthly fee instead of buying. A "SaaS fee" is that monthly rent.                                                                                                                                                                              |
| B2B / B2C / B2B2C                    | Selling to businesses / selling to consumers / selling to a business that then brings you its consumers (Juicy is B2B2C: it signs restaurants, who bring their customers).                                                                                             |
| TAM (total addressable market)       | Everyone in the world who could conceivably buy the product. The biggest number.                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| SAM (serviceable addressable market) | The part of the TAM this specific product can actually reach (right type of outlet, right city, right price).                                                                                                                                                          |
| SOM (serviceable obtainable market)  | The part of the SAM you can realistically win in the next few years, given competitors and your resources. The smallest and most honest number.                                                                                                                        |
| Bottom-up estimate                   | Counting from the ground: number of outlets × money per outlet, rather than taking a big industry number and guessing a percentage.                                                                                                                                    |
| CAC (customer acquisition cost)      | What it costs in marketing and sales effort to win one new customer (or one new restaurant).                                                                                                                                                                           |
| LTV (lifetime value)                 | The total profit one customer (or restaurant) brings you over the whole time they stay.                                                                                                                                                                                |
| LTV/CAC                              | Lifetime profit divided by the cost to win them. Below 1 you lose money on every customer; investors like 3 or more.                                                                                                                                                   |
| ARPU                                 | Average revenue per user (or per restaurant) per month or year.                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Churn                                | The share of customers (or restaurants) who stop using you in a period. 5% monthly churn means you lose a twentieth of your customers every month.                                                                                                                     |
| Retention                            | The opposite of churn: the share who are still using you after a week, a month, a year.                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Payback period                       | How many months it takes for a customer's profit to pay back what it cost to acquire them.                                                                                                                                                                             |
| Burn                                 | The cash the company loses every month after revenue. Runway is how many months of burn you can afford.                                                                                                                                                                |
| Break-even                           | The point where monthly revenue covers all monthly costs.                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Network effects                      | When each new user makes the product better for every existing user (more restaurants → more reasons for customers to open the app → more customers → more reasons for restaurants to join).                                                                           |
| Moat                                 | Whatever stops a competitor copying you and taking your customers: brand, data, contracts, switching cost, density.                                                                                                                                                    |
| Switching cost                       | The pain of moving to a competitor: re-entering menus, retraining staff, losing history.                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Multi-homing                         | A restaurant being on several platforms at the same time (Zomato AND Swiggy AND magicpin). It weakens every platform's hold.                                                                                                                                           |
| PMF (product-market fit)             | The moment people pull the product out of your hands: they come back without being reminded, tell friends, and complain when it breaks.                                                                                                                                |
| MVP (minimum viable product)         | The smallest version that lets you test the risky assumption. Not a polished product.                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Aha moment                           | The moment a new user first feels the value ("I walked past the queue and my food was waiting").                                                                                                                                                                       |
| NPS (net promoter score)             | A survey score from "would you recommend this to a friend?" (0–10). Promoters (9–10) minus detractors (0–6).                                                                                                                                                           |
| Concierge MVP                        | Doing the service by hand (for example taking orders on WhatsApp and typing them in yourself) before building software, to learn whether anyone wants it.                                                                                                              |
| Fake-door test                       | Showing a button or poster for a feature that does not exist yet, to count how many people try to use it.                                                                                                                                                              |
| Pilot                                | A small real-world trial with a few real outlets and real customers.                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| GTM (go-to-market)                   | The plan for how you will find and sign up customers: which city, which channel, which message.                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Channel                              | A way of reaching customers: Instagram, posters, campus ambassadors, WhatsApp groups, a sales person walking into shops.                                                                                                                                               |
| QSR                                  | Quick-service restaurant: fast food, counters, cafés; no table service.                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| POS (point of sale)                  | The billing machine or software at the counter (Petpooja and Posist are the big Indian ones).                                                                                                                                                                          |
| ONDC                                 | The Indian government-backed Open Network for Digital Commerce, which lets any app list any seller without a Zomato-style middleman.                                                                                                                                   |
| UPI                                  | India's instant bank-to-bank payment system (PhonePe, Google Pay, Paytm run on it).                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| MDR (merchant discount rate)         | The fee a shop pays on a digital payment. Zero for UPI below ₹2,000 by law.                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Payment aggregator (PA)              | A company licensed by the RBI to collect payments on behalf of many merchants (Razorpay, Cashfree, PhonePe PG, Paytm PG).                                                                                                                                              |
| Escrow / nodal account               | Money held by a licensed third party until a condition is met (for example until the food is collected).                                                                                                                                                               |
| KYC                                  | "Know your customer": the identity and bank paperwork a payment company needs before it can pay a merchant.                                                                                                                                                            |
| AOV (average order value)            | The average amount spent per order.                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| MTU / MAU / WAU / DAU                | Monthly transacting users / monthly, weekly, daily active users.                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Cohort                               | A group of users who started in the same week or month, tracked together to see how many stay.                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Runway                               | Months of cash left at the current burn.                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| Due diligence                        | The careful checking an investor does before putting money in. This report is that kind of check, done before anyone has invested.                                                                                                                                     |

## Part A: Executive summary and the direct answers

### The short version

1. **The problem is real but small, and nobody has measured it.** Queues at campus canteens and office counters at peak are a genuine, repeated annoyance. No Indian study, survey or news report measures how long they are. Every source that says "long queues" is a vendor selling a fix or a student proposing an app. The actual complaints from Bengaluru students and office workers are about food quality, portions and price.
2. **Order-ahead already works in India, in one shape only.** Bengaluru's own HungerBox (13 million orders a month, about 7.5 per user) and SmartQ (owned by Compass Group) run pre-ordering in captive cafeterias, sold to the employer or institution, with every vendor on it by default. Inside the open apps, pickup failed: Zomato and Swiggy both launched it in 2018, Zomato made it free for restaurants in 2020, and it still stayed at roughly 0.5 to 2.5% of Zomato's orders.
3. **Restaurants are angry, but not about this.** The July-August 2026 Bengaluru boycott threat against Zomato and Swiggy (1,000-plus owners) was about delivery commissions, ads and deductions, and it ended when the apps agreed terms. Owners stayed on the apps even at 40 to 45% total deductions, because that is where the customers are: they pay for demand, not for low fees. Pickup is already free (Zomato takeaway) or 1 to 3% (DotPe, Thrive, ONDC). Canteen contractors sell at college-fixed prices on 5 to 12% margins, so a 10% commission is their whole profit, and it becomes 15% if a 5% GST line has to be absorbed. Both research passes, working separately, named the 10% commission as the weakest single assumption in the plan.
4. **Customers will use it when the canteen makes it the default, not otherwise.** Phones, data and UPI are not the obstacle (94% Android, zero-fee UPI under ₹2,000, 24 billion UPI payments a month). Habit is: half of new apps are gone within 30 days, and order-ahead demand everywhere follows a daily route (Ritual lost 70% of its traffic when commuting stopped and never recovered). And price is: Swiggy's budget app Toing, aimed at students with prices that match or beat dine-in menus, reportedly reached about 33 million weekly users within a year, so Juicy can never be dearer than the counter.
5. **The market is a niche, not a platform.** Counting campuses only, the two passes landed on almost the same number: about ₹160 to 170 crore a year of platform revenue across India at a 10% commission, or ₹35 to 50 crore at the 2 to 3% outlets actually pay for pickup. Bengaluru is about ₹4 to 6 crore, of which a small team could win perhaps ₹0.2 to 1.2 crore a year. Adding offices and cafés raises the India figure to as much as ₹1,200 crore, but those segments are contested. The roadmap's own pilot numbers are ₹7 to 24 lakh a year.
6. **Everyone who tried this ended up somewhere else.** Tapingo sold to Grubhub and its app was folded into Grubhub's campus product (which DoorDash is now buying for $300 million); Snackpass pivoted to selling registers; Ritual collapsed; Allset and Yoyo closed; SmartQ was bought by Compass; HungerBox took a decade and 831 cafeterias to make ₹9 crore of profit. The survivors became the restaurant's software or the institution's vendor. None stayed an independent 10%-commission pickup app.
7. **The code is good; the order of work is wrong.** The app is well built for cheap phones on bad networks. But the roadmap schedules ten phases of platform engineering (multi-store, escrow ledger, event queues, cloud infrastructure) before a single café has paid for a single coffee. The next 30 days should reverse that.
8. **The roadmap's payment plan may not be available yet.** RBI rules forbid Juicy holding customers' money. The split-settlement product that escrow needs (Razorpay Route) now requires ₹40 lakh of prior turnover, and Cashfree's 0% offer does not cover its split product (the repository's own payment research). An e-commerce operator may also owe 5% GST on every canteen order. None of this blocks a pilot in which the outlet collects its own money, but all of it must be answered in writing before any real-money launch.

### The direct answers

**Do restaurants actually need Juicy?** General restaurants, no. A narrow set of captive-site counters (campus cafés, canteens, small office food courts) have a real peak-throughput problem, but the "need" belongs to the institution and the customer more than to the outlet, and the outlet will not pay 10% for it.

**Do customers actually need Juicy?** No, as a food app. Day-scholar students and staff at a captive outlet with a visible peak queue and a kitchen that can prepare ahead need the outcome Juicy promises, and they will use whatever ordering rail the outlet makes the default. Outside that setting Juicy is a subset of what Zomato and Swiggy already shipped and sidelined.

**What exact problem is valuable enough to pay for?** Peak-window throughput and cashless control in a captive site, paid for by the site owner (college, park operator, corporate). That is HungerBox's entire business. For a standalone café, converting the 20-minute rush into prepaid orders it would otherwise lose, worth perhaps ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month or ₹2 to 5 an order to the owner. Ratings, recommendations, analytics and surplus deals are features, not payers.

**Who should be the first paying customer?** One named, owner-run café or kiosk on one Bengaluru campus with a daily lunch queue you have timed yourself, free pricing, and a college willing to put the QR on the canteen wall, sold a flat monthly fee with the first month free. Second, the college itself, as a per-campus contract written into the next canteen tender. Not a canteen contractor on fixed prices, not a tech park already on HungerBox or SmartQ, not a cloud kitchen, not a dine-in restaurant.

**Why would a restaurant choose Juicy over existing alternatives?** Only if it visibly raises throughput at peak (more orders served per break, fewer walk-aways) and the fee is small enough to be paid out of that uplift. It is an operations sale with a measured before-and-after, not a "cheaper than Zomato" sale, because the orders Juicy brings come mostly from students who were already in the building. Honest differentiators: a web app that works on an ₹8,000 phone on bad Wi-Fi, prepaid with the outlet paid on no-show, no ads or forced discounts, stock-safe ordering of limited items.

**Why would a customer use Juicy instead of existing food platforms?** Only where the canteen pushes it: tokens called from the Juicy board, a dedicated pickup corner, minutes genuinely saved at peak, and the counter price with no fee or tax on top (Swiggy's Toing already offers students app prices at or below the counter). Without that in-venue enforcement the app will sit unused next to Zomato, exactly as takeaway did inside the aggregators.

**What is the biggest weakness in the current Juicy idea?** The business model. A 10% commission on a ₹200 pickup order from a customer who was already in the building, paid by an outlet with 5 to 12% margins, when the same thing is available free or at 1 to 3% elsewhere. Close behind: the roadmap builds the platform before validating the pilot.

**What is the biggest opportunity?** The long tail of uncontracted campuses and small institutions that HungerBox and SmartQ will not sell a managed-services contract to, and that Petpooja's QR add-on does not serve (no token calling, no prepayment rules, no no-show handling). Sold to the institution as "cashless, queue-free canteen", one contract at a time, it is the only version of this idea that has produced a profitable Indian company.

**What should I NOT build?** Multi-brand and multi-branch catalogs, store memberships, the escrow ledger and reconciliation service, the ETA engine, QR pickup verification, the recommender, analytics pipelines, cost-of-goods tracking, AWS migration, load testing to 1,000 users, Kubernetes, native app-store apps. None of it tests demand, and all of it is months of work. Also: do not build a discovery-by-area, many-restaurants consumer app.

**What should I build first?** Almost nothing. A "pause orders" switch, a chosen pickup time, and a way to tell a locked phone "your order is ready". Then run one outlet for four weeks with pay-at-counter or the outlet's own UPI QR, and measure.

**What would make Juicy difficult for competitors to copy?** Not the software. Default status on a campus (tokens called from the board, a pickup window, a campus wallet), density across every counter on that campus, deep POS and campus-ID integrations, and the money-handling rules (escrow until pickup, refunds when the outlet is at fault, paid on no-show, surplus resale) that QR tools skip. All of that is built by a sales team, campus by campus, over years.

**Can this realistically become a large company?** As a consumer food app competing with Swiggy and Zomato, no. As the ordering and payment rail of Indian campuses and institutions, possibly, and the exit, as for every comparable, would be to a facility operator, a POS company or an aggregator. That is a ₹50 to 100 crore-revenue outcome, roughly HungerBox's size today, after five or more years and ₹5 to 15 crore of capital, not a unicorn.

**What would need to be true for that to happen?** A measured peak queue of 8 minutes or more at target counters; 50% or more of a campus ordering through the app within a semester; institutions signing multi-year default-status contracts; a hybrid price clearing ₹5,000 or more per counter per month; a POS vendor or facility operator choosing to partner; patient capital and a founder willing to run a field-sales company for three years.

**What evidence should I collect before investing serious time or money?** Stopwatch queue measurements at five counters; fifteen owner interviews; a four-week single-outlet pilot showing the share of peak orders, week-4 retention and no-shows; the first rupee actually paid by an outlet; a chartered accountant's written view on whether a pickup-only platform must pay the 5% GST on every order; a written answer from Razorpay or Cashfree on whether a pre-revenue platform can use their split-settlement product; and whether Zomato and Swiggy still offer pickup on your target campus.

**What should I test in the next 30 days?** Week 1: measure the queue, interview, and send the payment and GST questions in writing; week 2: run one outlet by hand on WhatsApp; week 3: run it with the real app and pay-at-counter; week 4: ask for money and decide. Part E has the plan with pass and fail numbers.

### What the independent second opinion changed

The second pass, which never saw the first, reached the same core verdict: the problem is real but small, the 10% commission is the weakest part of the plan, the campus incumbents already sell this to institutions, and the realistic outcome is a small, flat-fee software business sold campus by campus. Its campus-only market estimate (about ₹170 crore a year for India at 10%) almost matches the first pass's (₹162 crore). It was more pessimistic in three places, and on each the evidence supports it: students have just been trained on price by Swiggy's Toing; a 5% GST line may make Juicy dearer than the counter; and the escrow product the roadmap depends on may not be open to a pre-revenue startup. It also made two factual slips that the comparison caught and corrected (Petpooja's price period, and a Domino's figure). Part D has the details.

## Part B: The due-diligence report (Pass 1)

This is the full study, in the order the brief asked for. Where the independent pass (Part C) later found a fact this pass missed, it has been added in place and marked, so this part is the most complete picture. Numbers are facts where linked, estimates where the arithmetic is shown, and assumptions where stated.

## 1. What Juicy actually is today (read from the code, not from the pitch)

Plain description. Juicy is a website that works like an app on a phone. A student opens it, sees the menu of a campus café with photos, taps items into a cart, "pays" (today the payment is only pretend; no real money moves), gets an order number, and watches the order go from "Placed" to "Preparing" to "Ready", then collects it at the counter. The café staff see a board of incoming orders on a phone or tablet and tap each order forward. An admin page holds a few site-wide settings.

What the code is good at. It is built carefully for cheap Android phones on slow campus Wi-Fi: it loads fast, caches the menu so it opens instantly on the next visit, keeps working through flaky networks, and never lets two people buy the last samosa at the same time (that race is settled inside the database). Security and accessibility were treated as requirements. This matters because most student-made ordering apps fall over at the first lunch rush.

What does not exist yet (and the roadmap is honest about this):

- No real payments. Taking real UPI money means signing with a licensed payment aggregator (Cashfree, Razorpay and others), finishing KYC paperwork, and deciding who legally sells the food. The roadmap has a whole research document on this.
- One café only. "Many restaurants, chains with branches, each with its own staff and hours" is planned but not built.
- No notifications. The customer has to keep the page open to see "Ready". A "your order is ready" ping (web push, SMS or WhatsApp) is planned.
- No restaurant-side menu editing, no analytics, no ratings, no deals, no QR pickup codes, no scheduled pickup times.
- No paying customer, no live outlet, no decided price. The roadmap's working example is a 10% commission per order.

Where the roadmap wants to go. A 33-item roadmap in ten phases: platform plumbing, multi-store, restaurant-managed menus, payment-aggregator selection, real payments with escrow and refunds, pickup tokens and QR, notifications, analytics and a recommender, AWS hosting, a load-tested pilot, then Kubernetes. The payment research assumes an average order of about ₹200, 99% paid by UPI, and pilot volumes of 3,000 to 10,000 orders a month.

One observation from the code before any market research. The roadmap is an engineering plan for a platform. It schedules months of work (multi-tenant data model, ledger, escrow, event queues, cloud infrastructure) before the first real customer has paid for a real coffee through it. Nothing in the repository yet records a conversation with a café owner, a count of people in a queue, or a price anyone agreed to pay. The market research below is partly about whether that order of work is right.

## 2. The problem: what Juicy fixes, and how much it hurts

### 2.1 Stated precisely

Juicy removes one step from a food counter: the ordering-and-paying step. It does not make the cooking faster, it does not deliver, and it does not help anyone discover where to eat. So the most time it can ever save a customer is the time they would have spent in the ordering line. If the real bottleneck at a canteen is the kitchen (one tawa, one fryer, one person at the coffee machine), then an order placed from the classroom still waits for the same tawa. The queue just moves from the counter to a "Ready" screen. Every conclusion below depends on this distinction, and the single most useful thing the founder can do this month is to find out, with a stopwatch, which of the two bottlenecks the target canteens actually have.

For the outlet, Juicy fixes two smaller things: shouted order-taking and cash-and-UPI reconciliation during the rush, and (once prepayment exists) the risk of making food for someone who never shows up.

### 2.2 How strong is the evidence that the pain exists?

Honest answer: the pain is claimed everywhere and measured nowhere. Research note 01 searched for any Indian study, survey or news report that measures how long students or office workers wait at a canteen at peak, and found none.

What does exist:

- A vendor guide from the Bengaluru campus-software company SpaceBasic (June 2026) says pre-ordering 30 to 60 minutes ahead cuts counter waits "from several minutes to under a minute" and cuts food waste by 20 to 35%. It names no institution and cites no independent source. [SpaceBasic, 1 Jun 2026](https://www.spacebasic.com/blogs/college-canteen-management-system)
- HungerBox's Bengaluru marketing page says tight lunch breaks in IT parks produce "long queues, order errors, cash handling headaches". Again, no numbers. [HungerBox](https://www.hungerbox.ai/blog/corporate-cafeteria-solution-bangalore/)
- A student paper from the Mumbai region (November 2023) says canteens "are often crowded during breaks, leading to long queues", and proposes an app. It contains no measurements. [Zenodo, Nov 2023](https://zenodo.org/records/10121675)
- The real complaints from real users that the research could find are about something else. An Azim Premji University survey of more than 800 students (February 2025) is about nutrition, portion size and cost of mess food; queues are not a headline complaint. [Akshayakalpa GFM, May 2025](https://gfm.akshayakalpa.org/read/feature-article/mess-on-my-plate-indias-students-are-fixing-their-college-diets) A Bengaluru office worker's post on the forum Blind (February 2026) complains about inedible cafeteria food and salads running out, not waiting. [Blind, 3 Feb 2026](https://www.teamblind.com/post/bangalore-office-food-is-actual-scam-and-feedback-process-doesnt-exist-cl196krz)

How long is the break the queue eats into? The Factories Act requires a rest of at least 30 minutes after five hours of work, and HR forums put common IT-company practice at 30 to 45 minutes. [Jibble, Oct 2025](https://www.jibble.io/labor-laws/india-labour-laws/break-laws) If a peak-hour ordering line takes 10 minutes (an assumption, because nobody has measured it), it eats a quarter to a third of the break. That is enough to annoy. Whether it is enough to change behaviour depends on what the alternatives cost, which is the next section.

### 2.3 The strongest evidence available: what people actually do today

Watching what people do beats asking what they would do. Three patterns stand out.

**Pattern 1: order-ahead already works in India, at scale, inside captive cafeterias, and it is sold to the employer, not to the diner.** HungerBox, headquartered in Bengaluru, claims 10.23 million orders a month across 735 Indian cafeterias and 1.36 million registered users (its global page says 891 cafeterias and 13.23 million orders). [HungerBox](https://www.hungerbox.ai/) That works out to roughly 7.5 orders per registered user per month: people in captive settings order through the system several times a week once it is the default way to pay. SmartQ, also from Bengaluru and owned by the catering giant Compass Group since March 2021, runs 264 food courts with an app, a web app of the exact kind Juicy is, kiosks, and "real-time notifications when their meal is ready". [SmartQ](https://www.thesmartq.com/smartq-tech-products) GoKhana claims 200,000 orders a day in the same segment. [GoKhana](https://www.gokhana.com/)

**Pattern 2: order-ahead inside a general food app has not caught on.** Zomato launched "Zomato Pickup" on 10 October 2018 in 13 cities including Bengaluru, with 20,000 restaurants. [The News Minute, Oct 2018](https://www.thenewsminute.com/atom/zomato-launches-option-pick-orders-restaurants-89756) Swiggy copied it 50 days later with "Swiggy Takeaway", first in Bengaluru. [Oneindia, Nov 2018](https://www.oneindia.com/india/now-swiggy-introduces-takeaway-food-services-for-users-in-bengaluru-2815036.html) In November 2020 Zomato made takeaway completely free for restaurants, waiving its commission and even the payment-gateway charge, with 55,000 restaurants live, and reported "tens of thousands" of takeaway orders a week. [Restaurant India, Nov 2020](https://www.restaurantindia.in/news/zomato-to-offer-takeaway-service-to-restaurants-at-zero-commission.n19543) Against roughly 3.8 million delivery orders a week at the time, that is about 0.5% to 2.5% of orders (an estimate from those two figures). Neither company mentions pickup in any investor document for the last two financial years. Whether the toggle still exists in the Bengaluru apps today could not be verified; it is a five-minute check the founder should do.

**Pattern 3: the money in Indian food is moving towards delivery, not away from it.** At Domino's India, the biggest chain, delivery rose from 68.9% to 72.9% of sales in one year, and the dine-in-plus-takeaway channel grew only 2.5%, "majorly driven by lunch hour meals". [Jubilant FoodWorks Q1 FY26 deck, Aug 2025](https://www.jubilantfoodworks.com/Uploads/Files/1221foodfile-EarningsPresentationandPR.pdf) McDonald's operator Westlife is spending to double delivery through its own app. [Reuters via Yahoo, Nov 2025](https://sg.finance.yahoo.com/news/mcdonalds-india-operator-launch-20-135559668.html) Zomato's food delivery grew 20% and Swiggy's 17% in the quarter to June 2026 (details in section 3).

Reading the three patterns together: order-ahead succeeds in India when it is the default rail of a captive cafeteria, installed by the institution, with every vendor on it. It has not succeeded as a feature consumers choose inside an open platform. Juicy's pilot setting (a campus canteen) matches the first, successful pattern. Juicy's roadmap ("many restaurants", "store discovery by area", chains with branches) drifts towards the second pattern, where the evidence is worst.

### 2.4 Party by party

| Who                                                      | How often                                    | How painful                                                                  | Solved today by                                                                           | Are they dissatisfied?                         | Would they change behaviour?                                                                                       | Verdict                                |
| -------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------- |
| Day-scholar students                                     | Every college day, at one or two breaks      | Time, not money. Coping: skip the canteen, go late, buy from a street vendor | Queue; pay by UPI QR or cash; a friend holds a place                                      | Asserted everywhere, measured nowhere          | Yes, if the saving is visible at peak and costs nothing extra; no, if the kitchen is the bottleneck                | Real, small, episodic                  |
| Hostel students                                          | Mess covers meals; canteen is snacks and tea | Low                                                                          | The mess                                                                                  | Unhappy with mess quality, not canteen queues  | Unlikely                                                                                                           | Nice-to-have                           |
| Office workers in big tech parks                         | Daily, 30 to 45 minute lunch                 | Moderate                                                                     | Usually HungerBox or SmartQ already                                                       | Complaints are about quality and price         | They already switched, to the incumbent                                                                            | Already served                         |
| Office workers in small offices                          | Daily                                        | Moderate                                                                     | Walk to a darshini or café; delivery                                                      | Delivery fees irritate                         | Only if several nearby outlets are on one app, which is a discovery product Juicy is not                           | Unproven                               |
| Campus cafés and canteens                                | Every peak                                   | High at peak: shouted orders, cash reconciliation, wrong orders, waste       | Counter plus tokens; some institutions buy a canteen module from a campus-software vendor | Likely yes at peak                             | Only if it does not cost them margin: ₹20 of commission on a ₹200 order is most of the profit on a low-margin item | The real customer, but price-sensitive |
| Small local restaurants                                  | Peak only                                    | Their pain is aggregator commissions, not queues                             | Walk-in, phone, WhatsApp, Zomato, Swiggy                                                  | Yes, with aggregator economics                 | They will list anywhere free; they will not pay 10% for pickup orders they already take by phone for free          | Nice-to-have                           |
| Café chains (Third Wave, Blue Tokai, Chaayos, Starbucks) | Peak coffee hours                            | Low; they have their own apps, and Starbucks has had order-ahead since 2020  | Own app plus aggregators                                                                  | No evidence                                    | No                                                                                                                 | Not a customer                         |
| Cloud kitchens                                           | Never: no walk-in by design                  | None                                                                         | Delivery platforms                                                                        | With commissions, yes; with pickup, irrelevant | No                                                                                                                 | Not a customer                         |
| Food courts and malls                                    | Weekend and lunch peaks                      | Moderate, but visitors have time                                             | Tokens, POS, some QR systems (HungerBox did mall food courts in 2020)                     | No data                                        | The mall operator decides, not the tenant: a business-to-business sale                                             | Unproven                               |
| Large restaurant chains                                  | Peak                                         | Low: Domino's app has 14.7 million monthly users                             | Own app plus aggregators                                                                  | Dissatisfied with aggregators, hence own apps  | No                                                                                                                 | Not a customer                         |

**Nice-to-have versus high-value.** The problem is high-value for exactly one pairing: a captive canteen with a visible peak queue and a kitchen that can prepare ahead (idli-vada, sandwiches, tea, bakery items, pre-plated thalis), together with its day-scholar students or staff. Everyone else either has no queue, has a kitchen bottleneck that order-ahead cannot fix, or already has a system.

## 3. The customer side: do people actually want this?

### 3.1 What Indians do today

- **Eating out is cheap, frequent and growing.** The restaurant association's India Food Services Report 2024 says people dine out 3.7 times and order in 4.2 times a month on average (7.9 occasions); Delhi consumers spend about ₹1,165 a month eating out. [Millennium Post on NRAI, Jul 2024](https://www.millenniumpost.in/delhi/ncr-has-the-food-market-size-of-rs-42000-cr-states-nrai-report-571403) Dividing those gives roughly ₹130 per occasion (an estimate): everyday eating out is cheap, which makes a ₹200 prepaid canteen order normal, maybe even a little high for canteen food.
- **Delivery is the digital habit.** In the quarter to June 2026, Zomato's food delivery had 27.2 million monthly transacting customers and grew 20% in order value; Swiggy had 19.2 million and grew 17%. [Medianama on Eternal Q1 FY27, Jul 2026](https://www.medianama.com/2026/07/223-takeaways-eternal-q1-fy27-earnings-call/); [IIFL on Swiggy Q1 FY27, Jul 2026](https://www.indiainfoline.com/news/earnings/swiggy-share-price-slip-3-after-q1-fy27-results-revenue-jumps-37-net-loss-narrows) Redseer puts delivery frequency at 4.5 to 5 orders per user per month (press-reported). A Swiggy user spends roughly ₹1,650 a month on the app (an estimate from order value divided by users).
- **What users resent about delivery is cost.** Platform fees rose "as much as 400%" in a year and delivery got 20 to 30% costlier between 2023 and 2024. [Inc42, Feb 2025](https://inc42.com/features/zomato-swiggy-food-delivery-india-dead-end/) That is the one emotional lever a pickup product has: no delivery fee, no platform fee.
- **The phone and the network are not obstacles.** Android is 93.9% of Indian mobile; iPhones 6%. [Statcounter, Sep 2026](https://gs.statcounter.com/os-market-share/mobile/india) Mobile data costs about US$0.16 per gigabyte, among the cheapest in the world, and Indians use about 31 GB a month. [Nokia MBiT 2026](https://nenews.in/tech/indias-average-mobile-data-usage-crosses-31-gb-per-month-report/46452/) UPI handled 24.51 billion transactions in August 2026, and more than 95% of merchant payments are ₹2,000 or less, where there is no fee. [Entrackr, Sep 2026](https://entrackr.com/news/upi-hits-new-record-with-2451-bn-transactions-in-august-12478578); [SCC Online on NPCI FAQ, Sep 2026](https://www.scconline.com/blog/post/2026/09/16/npci-released-upi-mdr-faqs-explained/) Juicy's decision to build for cheap Android phones on slow networks is the right engineering choice for this user, not over-engineering.

### 3.2 Segment by segment

| Segment                                       | Today                                                                                            | Biggest frustration                               | Values most                                                          | Is Juicy meaningfully better?                                             | Would switch if                                                                        | Would stop if                                                                                                        |
| --------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| College students (day scholars)               | Canteen walk-up with UPI QR; Zomato or Swiggy in the evening; WhatsApp with local tiffin sellers | The queue at the break; canteen quality and price | Speed inside the break, same price as the counter, not missing class | Yes at peak, if the canteen prepares ahead and calls tokens; otherwise no | The saving is obvious the first time: walk past the queue, token called, same price    | The order is late even once (prepaid, trapped, waiting twice), a refund drags, or staff serve the walk-up line first |
| Hostel students                               | Mess, canteen for snacks                                                                         | Mess quality                                      | Variety, cost                                                        | Marginal                                                                  |                                                                                        |                                                                                                                      |
| Office workers in large campuses              | HungerBox or SmartQ wallets and pre-order                                                        | Quality, vendors running out                      | Variety, quality, speed                                              | No: the incumbent already does pre-order and ready notifications          | Their employer switches vendor, which is a business sale                               |                                                                                                                      |
| Office workers in small offices               | Walk to nearby outlets; delivery                                                                 | Fees; short lunch                                 | Speed, price                                                         | Only if several outlets near the office are on Juicy                      | The outlet they already use puts a QR on the counter and the order is ready on arrival | The app is slower than walking                                                                                       |
| Families                                      | Delivery, dining out, mall food courts                                                           | Delivery fees, weekend crowds                     | Convenience, quality                                                 | No: not delivery, not dine-in                                             |                                                                                        |                                                                                                                      |
| Frequent delivery orderers (Gold, Swiggy One) | 5 or more delivery orders a month; membership for free delivery                                  | Minimum order values, surge, fees                 | Discounts, breadth                                                   | No: they want food brought to the desk or door                            | Pickup is cheaper by more than the walk is worth                                       |                                                                                                                      |
| People dining out                             | District, Swiggy Dineout, walk-in                                                                | Discount culture                                  | Discovery, deals, ambience                                           | No: Juicy is not dine-in                                                  |                                                                                        |                                                                                                                      |
| Budget-conscious                              | Walk-in, cash or UPI, shared orders                                                              | Any fee at all                                    | Price                                                                | Yes in principle (no fees), but only at counter price                     | Price parity and no fee                                                                | Any convenience fee or a worse price than the counter                                                                |
| Premium (Starbucks, Third Wave, Blue Tokai)   | Brand apps, loyalty                                                                              | Little                                            | Brand, consistency                                                   | No                                                                        |                                                                                        |                                                                                                                      |
| Food-court and mall users                     | Walk-in, tokens                                                                                  | Weekend crowds                                    | Variety, seating                                                     | Marginal: leisure time blunts the queue pain                              | The mall deploys it everywhere                                                         |                                                                                                                      |
| Cloud-kitchen users                           | Delivery only                                                                                    | Fees                                              | Price, speed                                                         | No                                                                        |                                                                                        |                                                                                                                      |

### 3.3 Would they really use another food app?

- **App fatigue is real but not fatal.** Globally, 46.1% of apps installed in 2024 were uninstalled within 30 days, most on day one. [AppsFlyer uninstall report, 2025](https://www.appsflyer.com/resources/reports/app-uninstall-benchmarks-report/) In India, shopping-app installs grew 85% in two years while day-30 retention fell 41% between late 2025 and mid 2026. [BestMediaInfo on AppsFlyer India, Sep 2026](https://bestmediainfo.com/insights/india-shopping-app-installs-jump-85-but-user-retention-falls-41-12567179) Translation: people will try a new app, and most will drop it within a month unless it becomes a habit.
- **Juicy's "website that installs to the home screen" choice removes the install barrier** (no app store, no 50 MB download on a metered plan). It gives up app-store discovery and, on iPhones, some notification reliability, but iPhones are 6% of the Indian market. SmartQ already ships a web app alongside its native app for exactly this use, so the format is proven.
- **Habit needs a daily trigger.** A canteen visited every college day is a daily trigger. A random restaurant across town is not. This is the single strongest argument for Juicy staying a campus product rather than becoming a city-wide food app.

### 3.4 How many students are there in Bengaluru?

No official district-level figure exists. Bengaluru Urban has 1,148 colleges, the most of any district in India, out of Karnataka's 4,529 ([AISHE 2023-24](https://www.dohe-education.gov.in/static/uploads/2026/07/8616f33dfee644ab6b87a2bd0658b18d.pdf); the figure was 1,106 in [AISHE 2021-22](https://cdnbbsr.s3waas.gov.in/s392049debbe566ca5782a3045cf300a3c/uploads/2024/02/20240719952688509.pdf)). Karnataka enrols 2.76 million students in higher education. [Educationist on AISHE 2023-24, Aug 2026](https://educationist.com/article/indias-higher-education-system-reaches-45-million-students-nep-reforms-show-uneven-progress) Applying the college share (25%) to the state enrolment gives about 700,000 students; because Bengaluru's institutions are larger than the state average and the city hosts most of the private and deemed universities, the honest range is about 0.7 to 1.1 million students (the research workstreams' own estimates ranged across exactly that span).

What the roadmap's own pilot numbers mean: 3,000 orders a month at ₹200 is ₹6 lakh of food a month; 10,000 orders is ₹20 lakh. At a 10% commission that is ₹60,000 to ₹2 lakh a month of platform revenue before any costs. If an active student orders about 10 times a month, those volumes need only 300 to 1,000 active orderers, which is 3 to 20% of a 5,000-student campus. These are small numbers in both directions: achievable, and not a business at one campus.

### 3.5 Direct answer: do customers need Juicy, or is it just another food app?

**Evidence that the need is real.** Captive cafeterias already run on order-ahead at scale (HungerBox's ~7.5 orders per user per month). The lunch hour is a live occasion even at delivery-first chains (Domino's). Everyday eating out is cheap and frequent, and UPI makes a ₹200 prepayment frictionless. Delivery fees are the one thing users visibly resent, and pickup has none. The phones and networks are ready.

**Evidence against.** Nobody has measured the queue, and the two real-user complaints found about Bengaluru campus food are about quality, not waiting. The two giants built pickup in 2018, Zomato made it free for restaurants in 2020, and neither mentions it now. Swiggy's Toing, a budget app aimed at students and first jobbers with prices that "match or beat dine-in menus", reportedly reached about 33 million weekly users within a year (a CLSA estimate found by the independent pass, Part C), so students now expect app prices at or below the counter. The money is moving towards delivery. Where the pain is biggest (large offices) it is already solved by incumbents paid by the employer. Where it is unsolved (colleges) the money per order is tiny and students' time is cheap in rupee terms, which is exactly why nobody has monetised it. Half of new apps are gone in a month. And prepayment creates a new failure mode: a walk-up customer who sees a long line can leave, but a prepaid customer whose order is late is trapped and angry, and a UPI refund is not instant.

**Verdict.** Customers do not "need Juicy". A narrow group, day scholars and staff at a captive outlet with a visible peak queue and a kitchen that can prepare ahead, need the outcome Juicy promises, and they will use whatever ordering rail the outlet makes the default. In that setting Juicy is not "another food app"; it is the canteen's own ordering system, and its real comparison is HungerBox and SmartQ, not Zomato. Outside that setting Juicy is a pickup-only subset of what Zomato and Swiggy shipped in 2018 and quietly stopped talking about. The problem is real, small, episodic and unmeasured: high-value for the canteen at 12:30, nice-to-have for the student at 12:30, and irrelevant at 4 pm.

## 4. The restaurant side: what outlets suffer, what they already use, and who decides

### 4.1 What restaurants pay the delivery apps today (the pain is real, and it is a delivery pain)

The two listed aggregators publish their numbers every quarter. For the quarter to June 2026:

|                                                                | Zomato (Eternal)                                                   | Swiggy                                              |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------- |
| Food-delivery order value                                      | ₹10,769 crore net of discounts, up 20%                             | ₹9,490 crore gross, up 17.4%                        |
| The platform's revenue as a share of order value ("take rate") | 32.8% (adjusted revenue ÷ net order value, as the company reports) | 23.3% (operating revenue ÷ order value)             |
| Operating profit on order value                                | 5.6%                                                               | 3.1% (contribution margin 7.6%)                     |
| Monthly users / restaurants                                    | 27.2 million customers; 328,000 restaurants                        | 19.2 million users; 265,800 transacting restaurants |

Sources: [Eternal Q1 FY27 letter](https://b.zmtcdn.com/investor-relations/Eternal_Limited_Shareholders_Letter_Q1FY27_Results.pdf); [Swiggy Q1 FY27 letter](https://www.swiggy.com/corporate/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/Q1-FY2027-Shareholder-letter.pdf); [Inc42, Sep 2026](https://inc42.com/features/swiggys-money-map-the-verticals-built-around-food-delivery-core/). Swiggy's own data book shows its take rate rising every year, from 24.0% in FY22 to 26.0% in the latest quarter. [Swiggy KPI book](https://www.swiggy.com/corporate/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/KPI-Book_Q1FY27.xlsx)

What an individual restaurant pays per delivery order: a commission of 18 to 30% (Swiggy's own description on its August 2026 call; chains negotiate 10 to 22%), plus 18% GST on that commission, plus about 2% payment-gateway fees, plus 0.1% tax deducted at source, plus discounts and ads it funds. The Bengaluru hotel association's worked example: a ₹400 dish nets the restaurant about ₹220. [The Week, 30 Jul 2026](https://www.theweek.in/news/biz-tech/2026/07/30/wont-take-orders-anymore-why-bengaluru-restaurants-are-threatening-to-boycott-zomato-swiggy.html) Associations say a restaurant keeps only 40 to 55% of the bill.

Other grievances with evidence:

- **Ads and discounts charged without consent.** In June 2025 restaurateurs alleged Swiggy ads "morphed, without consent, into 33% deductions"; one 16-outlet group said ₹1.4 lakh was deducted in four to five months. Swiggy said under 1% of partners were affected. [Outlook Business, Jun 2025](https://www.outlookbusiness.com/news/restaurants-allege-zomato-swiggy-charge-them-for-ads-discounts-without-consent)
- **Customer-side fees keep rising.** Zomato's platform fee went from ₹2 in August 2023 to ₹14.90 in March 2026; Swiggy's to ₹17.58. [Business Standard, Mar 2026](https://www.business-standard.com/companies/news/swiggy-platform-fee-hike-zomato-price-increase-food-delivery-india-costs-126032400608_1.html) Customers now tolerate a ₹15 to 18 flat fee on a ₹300 to 400 order. That matters for Juicy's pricing (section 5).
- **Customer data.** The decade-old complaint is being partly answered: since November 2025 both apps share customer phone numbers with restaurants on opt-in. [Medianama, Nov 2025](https://www.medianama.com/2025/11/223-zomato-swiggy-share-customer-data-restaurants/)
- **Payouts.** Zomato pays weekly, and daily (T+3) for small outlets since December 2024. [Inc42, Dec 2024](https://inc42.com/buzz/zomato-rolls-out-daily-payout-for-select-restaurants/) Note for Juicy: "hold the restaurant's money until pickup" settles slower than that unless settlement is same-day.

### 4.2 The Bengaluru pushback of 2026

| Date              | Event                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| ----------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Jan 2025          | The restaurant association NRAI says it will go to the competition regulator over the apps' private-label kitchens and data use, and pushes ONDC as an alternative                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| Jun 2025          | Allegations of unconsented ads and discounts (above)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Nov 2025          | Swiggy adds a per-order charge on restaurants for Swiggy One orders; opt-in phone-number sharing begins                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Mar 2026          | Platform fees raised to ₹14.90 (Zomato) and ₹17.58 (Swiggy)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| 23 Jul 2026       | The CCI dismisses an individual's complaint about Zomato's fees and commissions: no case                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| 29-30 Jul 2026    | The Bruhat Bengaluru Hotels Association and three other bodies, backed by more than 1,000 owners, threaten to delist from Swiggy and Zomato from 15 August. Demands: itemised deductions, no unapproved discounts, pay for cancelled prepared orders, a 22% commission cap for new partners. [Business Standard, 30 Jul 2026](https://www.business-standard.com/industry/news/bengaluru-restaurants-threaten-swiggy-zomato-boycott-what-s-the-dispute-126073000471_1.html)                                                                    |
| 6-7 Aug 2026      | After meetings with both CEOs the platforms agreed terms: promotions only with the restaurant's OTP consent, refunds of unauthorised campaign charges, and an MoU; the deadline moved to 31 August. [Financial Express, 7 Aug 2026](https://www.financialexpress.com/business/news/food-delivery-platforms-agree-terms-with-bengaluru-hotel-bodies-to-avert-boycott/4313276/); [Oneindia](https://www.oneindia.com/bengaluru/bengaluru-hotels-defer-swiggy-zomato-boycott-till-august-31-give-platforms-more-time-after-ceo-tal-8170647.html) |
| After 31 Aug 2026 | No source found reports that the boycott went ahead. (Pass 1 could not find the outcome; the independent pass found the agreement above.)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                     |

What it means for Juicy: the anger is real, organised and centred on Bengaluru, which proves owners will listen to a lower-commission pitch. But every demand on the list is about delivery-order economics. None is "we need a pickup channel". And the apps conceded enough within three weeks to defuse it. Owners also stayed on the apps even at 40 to 45% total deductions, because "that's where the customers are" ([Mint, Sep 2025](https://www.livemint.com/industry/restaurants-food-delivery-apps-margins-swiggy-zomato-bulk-orders-aggregator-platforms-11758455461794.html), found by the independent pass): restaurants pay for demand, not for low fees.

### 4.3 What a restaurant can already use for pickup, and what it costs

| Alternative                                         | What the restaurant pays                                                                                                                                                | Evidence                                                                                                                                                                      |
| --------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Zomato takeaway                                     | 0% commission, gateway fee absorbed by Zomato (announced November 2020; 2026 terms unverified)                                                                          | [Restaurant India, Nov 2020](https://www.restaurantindia.in/news/zomato-to-offer-takeaway-service-to-restaurants-at-zero-commission.n19543)                                   |
| Swiggy Toing (budget app, 50 cities)                | Lower, undisclosed commission; Swiggy says zero commission is unsustainable                                                                                             | [Medianama, Aug 2026](https://www.medianama.com/2026/08/223-swiggy-earnings-call-key-takeaways/)                                                                              |
| ONDC via magicpin                                   | Promotional 0%, typically 2 to 5% (secondary sources disagree with NRAI's "10 to 11%"); the customer pays a ₹5 platform fee                                             | [Business Standard, Oct 2024](https://www.business-standard.com/companies/start-ups/magicpin-largest-food-delivery-app-on-ondc-logs-150-000-orders-daily-124100800892_1.html) |
