| Rapido Ownly (Bengaluru, citywide since March 2026) | 0% commission, ₹30 flat delivery fee; about 20,000 Bengaluru restaurants                                                                                                | [Medianama, Mar 2026](https://www.medianama.com/2026/03/223-rapido-ownly-bengaluru-zero-commission-model-sustain-without-charging-restaurants/)                               |
| DotPe                                               | "very low single digits" (2021); 1 to 3% per secondary sources                                                                                                          | [DotPe](https://dotpe.in/)                                                                                                                                                    |
| Thrive                                              | About 3% per order; ₹1,000 to 5,000 a month plans                                                                                                                       | [Razorpay listing](https://razorpay.com/app-store/thrive/)                                                                                                                    |
| UrbanPiper                                          | ₹2,000 to 3,000 a month (secondary)                                                                                                                                     | [Forkcast comparison](https://www.forkcast.in/blog/petpooja-vs-urbanpiper-vs-posist)                                                                                          |
| Petpooja                                            | List price per outlet, ex-tax: ₹12,000 base; ₹20,000 for the plan that adds token management, QR ordering and a kitchen screen (third parties describe these as annual) | [Petpooja pricing](https://www.petpooja.com/pricing), read 1 Oct 2026; the page itself does not say monthly or yearly (Part D)                                                |
| Posist / Restroworks                                | ₹2,500 to 5,000 per outlet per month plus setup (secondary)                                                                                                             |                                                                                                                                                                               |

So a restaurant that wants pickup ordering today can switch on Zomato takeaway for free, pay its POS vendor about ₹20,000 a year for QR and token ordering, or pay 2 to 5% to a direct-ordering tool that also brings delivery. Juicy's roadmap commission (10%, configurable up to 30%) sits above all of these except the aggregators themselves.

### 4.4 Restaurant margins

No audited margin series for Indian independents exists. Industry rules of thumb: net profit margins of 5 to 15%, with rent the biggest factor; a 2026 vendor guide gives 5 to 12% typical, food cost 28 to 35%, rent ideally under 10 to 15%, labour under 25%. [Restaurant India, May 2024](https://www.restaurantindia.in/article/how-profitable-is-restaurant-business-in-india.14000); [DineOpen guide, 2026](https://www.dineopen.com/blog/restaurant-profit-margins-india-guide.html) (a vendor blog) Why this matters: at an 8% net margin, a 10% commission on a ₹200 order (₹20) is more than the restaurant's entire profit on that order (₹16). A commission is only rational on orders that are new, or that replace a costlier aggregator order. A queue-skipping campus order is mostly a walk-in customer moved to a phone.

### 4.5 The operational problem list, and whether Juicy touches it

| Problem                                                 | How severe, with evidence                                                                                                                                              | Who feels it                                       | Does Juicy address it?                                                                 |
| ------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Aggregator commission 18 to 30% plus GST                | Severe; the 2026 boycott                                                                                                                                               | Delivery-dependent restaurants, cloud kitchens     | No: Juicy is pickup only, and Zomato takeaway is already free                          |
| Ads and discounts deducted without consent              | Severe for those hit                                                                                                                                                   | Same                                               | Indirectly: Juicy has no ads or forced discounts, but brings no delivery demand either |
| Customer-data ownership                                 | Moderate; now partly solved by opt-in sharing                                                                                                                          | Restaurants with a repeat local base               | Yes: Juicy owns the relationship                                                       |
| Payout delay and cash flow                              | Real for small outlets                                                                                                                                                 | Small outlets                                      | Worse, not better, unless escrow settles same-day                                      |
| Queue and peak-hour load                                | Real in captive sites: HungerBox's 13 million orders a month exist because of it; SpaceBasic: "the worst canteen experience happens in the 20 minutes between classes" | Canteens, food courts, campus cafés                | Yes: the core product                                                                  |
| Order management and kitchen tokens                     | Petpooja's ₹20,000 plan already includes tokens and a kitchen screen                                                                                                   | Any counter                                        | Partly: a staff board; no POS or kitchen-screen integration                            |
| Inventory and wastage                                   | HungerBox claims forecasting cuts waste 18 to 22%; cafeteria pilferage runs 8 to 10% of revenue (a 2018 account says 18 to 22% in cash systems)                        | Canteen operators and the institutions paying them | Partly: stock counts; surplus deals on the roadmap                                     |
| Cash leakage and transparency                           | Real; the institution wants it, the contractor's staff often do not. HungerBox faced "backlash from vendors" over transparency in 2018                                 | Institutions                                       | Yes, by design (prepaid), which is also why contractors may resist                     |
| No-shows on pre-orders                                  | Not quantified anywhere public                                                                                                                                         | Any pre-order counter                              | Yes: prepaid, outlet paid on no-show (roadmap)                                         |
| Reviews, POS integration, table and QR dine-in ordering | Owned by Petpooja, Posist, UrbanPiper, DotPe                                                                                                                           | Dine-in outlets                                    | No                                                                                     |

### 4.6 Campus canteens: how they are actually run

This is the planned first market, and it works differently from a restaurant. College canteens are usually run by a contractor chosen by tender, who pays the college a licence fee and sells at prices fixed by a college committee. A primary tender document makes the economics concrete. Deshbandhu College, University of Delhi, 2024-25: about 5,500 students and 400 staff; a one-year licence at ₹10,000 a month; a ₹75,000 deposit; prices fixed by the committee (ginger tea ₹8, coffee ₹10, samosa ₹8); "the canteen contractor shall not tamper with the rates once agreed to", with a fine of up to ₹5,000; the committee "shall monitor it on daily basis"; the college can terminate on a month's notice; and no requirement for digital payment at all. [Deshbandhu College canteen tender 2024-25 (PDF)](https://www.deshbandhucollege.ac.in/uploads/pdf/1722328816_canteentender.pdf)

Arithmetic from that document: if 30% of the college's 5,900 people buy something daily at an average of ₹40, the canteen turns over about ₹70,000 a day, roughly ₹17 lakh a month. The licence is under 1% of sales. A 10% commission on all sales would be ₹1.7 lakh a month, which is roughly the contractor's entire profit at a 10% net margin. Because prices are fixed by the committee, the contractor cannot pass a commission on to students. The only fee a contractor could rationally accept is one on a small slice of orders, or one so small it is noise.

Who decides: the college administration or canteen committee controls who runs the canteen, what it charges, and whether it survives. The contractor is the user; the college is the buyer. The campus-software vendor SpaceBasic says the same thing from the seller's side: the institution makes the software decision, and it sells "SaaS subscription, typically per student per year" with a closed-loop wallet.

Cash versus UPI: no survey of canteen payment mix exists. UPI QR at the counter is probably near-universal in urban colleges (more than 95% of UPI merchant payments are under ₹2,000), but prepaid app ordering is not.

### 4.7 Corporate cafeterias and tech-park food courts

The corporate model is well documented because two Bengaluru companies built it. HungerBox: about 10% commission on vendor food sales plus management fees from corporates, "operationally profitable" by 2018; today 891 cafeterias, 13.23 million orders a month, and a case study of a Bengaluru "educational institute" with 15,000 cafeteria users. [HungerBox](https://www.hungerbox.ai/); [Outlook Business, Jul 2020](https://www.outlookbusiness.com/enterprise/feature/corporate-cafeterias-were-readying-to-take-off-when-it-caught-the-chills); [Business Today, Jun 2018](https://www.businesstoday.in/latest/corporate/story/how-hungerbox-makes-digitalised-cafeterias-work-in-india-148835-2018-06-12) SmartQ (Compass): 400 corporate sites, 7 lakh daily users, pricing "fully customised based on headcount, meal sessions, location". Rebel Foods pays 10% to list on HungerBox. Cafeteria pilferage runs 8 to 10% of revenue, and digitisation saves vendors "25 to 30%" of working capital. The corporate's admin or facilities team decides; vendors are told, not asked.

Tech-park food courts (Manyata, Bagmane, Embassy, Whitefield, Electronic City): no public data on vendor rent or revenue-share terms. The closest proxy is the mall food court, where tenants pay 15 to 25% of gross revenue on top of rent (secondary blogs), and an older account of 28% of net sales at Mantri Mall. A further commission on top of that is a hard sell; a flat fee for a shared pre-order layer is plausible.

### 4.8 Who already sells pre-ordering into campuses

| Player                    | Segment                          | What they charge                                                               | Visible traction                                                             |
| ------------------------- | -------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| HungerBox college product | Corporate and college, Bengaluru | Not published for colleges; corporate model 8 to 10% of vendor sales plus fees | GITAM, ICFAI, Oberoi International named; tokens, pre-order, UPI, meal cards |
| SmartQ / Compass          | Corporate, some universities     | Custom                                                                         | 400 sites; IIM Bangalore listed                                              |
| SpaceBasic                | Colleges and hostels, Bengaluru  | Per student per year                                                           | PES, Reva, Manipal, NITTE named; FY25 revenue ₹2.2 crore                     |
| MealPe                    | Colleges, IT parks; Ahmedabad    | "Subscription plus commission plus premium features"; no rates published       | Pre-seed; no campuses or orders published; counters read zero                |
| SkipQ                     | Colleges; Ghaziabad              | None published                                                                 | No colleges published                                                        |
| Biiite ("Skip the Queue") | Colleges; Ghaziabad              | None published                                                                 | 100-plus downloads on Google Play, updated September 2026                    |
| Student GitHub projects   |                                  |                                                                                | Dozens of "skip the canteen queue" apps                                      |

Reading: the idea is obvious enough that students build it as a college project; the three startups that productised it show no public traction; the two companies with real scale sell to institutions and bundle managed services. Juicy would be the fourth small entrant in the college niche, two to three years behind, with no payments live.

### 4.9 Which problems are worth paying for, and who adopts first

Severe enough to pay for, with evidence:

1. **Peak-window throughput in a captive site with a short break.** This is HungerBox's and SmartQ's entire business, paid for by corporates and tolerated by vendors.
2. **Cash leakage and transparency.** 8 to 22% pilferage; the institution and the owner gain, the counter staff and sometimes the contractor resist.
3. Aggregator commissions: severe, but a delivery problem, not a pickup one.
4. Working capital: real, but prepaid-with-escrow does not help unless settlement is same-day.

Not severe enough on their own: customer data, reviews, "order again", analytics (every POS sells it).

Who adopts first:

- **First:** an owner-run café or kiosk inside or at the gate of a campus of 2,000 to 15,000 people, with its own menu prices, two or three daily rushes, and regulars it recognises. It can price in a small fee, it feels the rush personally, and no HungerBox contract covers it. The college's blessing (QR posters on the wall, the Wi-Fi allow-list) is the real distribution channel.
- **Second:** a college administration buying "cashless, queue-free canteen" as an amenity for a flat annual fee, imposed on its contractor through the next tender.
- **Third:** small tech-park or co-working food counters too small for HungerBox or SmartQ to bother with.
- **Last or never:** dine-in restaurants, cloud kitchens, canteen contractors on committee-fixed prices (no margin to give), tech parks already on HungerBox or SmartQ, and any outlet already on Zomato takeaway or a POS with QR ordering.

### 4.10 Direct answers

**Do restaurants actually need Juicy?** General restaurants do not. A narrow set of captive-site counters have a real throughput problem, but the "need" there belongs to the institution and the customer more than to the outlet, and the outlet will not pay 10% for it.

**What exact problem is valuable enough to pay for?** Peak-window throughput and cashless control in a captive site, paid for by the site owner. For a standalone café, converting the 20-minute rush into prepaid orders it would otherwise lose, which it might pay ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month or ₹2 to 5 an order for. Everything else on the roadmap (ratings, recommender, analytics, surplus deals) is a feature, not a payer.

**Who should be the first paying customer?** A named, single, owner-run café on one Bengaluru campus with a daily lunch queue the founder has timed, free pricing, and a college willing to put the QR on the wall, sold a flat monthly fee with the first month free to measure how many orders actually move. Second, the college itself, as a per-campus contract written into the next canteen tender.

**Why would a restaurant choose Juicy over the alternatives?** Honest differentiators: a web app built for an ₹8,000 to 15,000 Android phone on bad campus Wi-Fi, which no incumbent optimises for; prepaid with the outlet paid on no-show; no ads, no forced discounts; stock-safe ordering for limited items. Reasons they would not: no native app; payments are simulated; no POS integration; no customers yet; a higher commission than DotPe, Thrive or ONDC; Zomato takeaway is free; HungerBox already has a college product. On today's evidence an outlet chooses Juicy only if it is cheaper than the alternatives and pushed by its institution.

## 5. Willingness to pay: what the market already charges, and which pricing models could work

### 5.1 The price anchors (what Indian outlets and customers already pay)

| Benchmark                                 | Who pays              | Amount                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| ----------------------------------------- | --------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Zomato / Swiggy commission (2026)         | Restaurant            | 18 to 30% plus 18% GST on the commission; chains 10 to 22%                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Zomato / Swiggy platform fee (March 2026) | Customer              | ₹14.90 / ₹17.58 per order                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Zomato takeaway (2020)                    | Restaurant            | 0%, gateway absorbed                                                                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| magicpin on ONDC                          | Customer / restaurant | ₹5 per order / 0% promotional, 2 to 5% typical                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| HungerBox (2018 to 2020)                  | Vendor plus corporate | 8 to 10% of vendor sales plus a management fee; works out to about ₹5.9 of revenue per order in FY25                                                                                                                                                              |
| DotPe / Thrive                            | Restaurant            | Low single digits / about 3%; ₹1,000 to 5,000 a month plans                                                                                                                                                                                                       |
| Petpooja (list, October 2026)             | Restaurant            | ₹12,000 base; ₹20,000 with QR, tokens and kitchen screen; ₹20,000 to 40,000 for the marketing suite (per outlet, ex-tax; third parties say annual)                                                                                                                |
| Posist / Restroworks                      | Restaurant            | ₹2,500 to 5,000 per outlet per month plus setup                                                                                                                                                                                                                   |
| SpaceBasic                                | Institution           | Per student per year; at most about ₹2.2 lakh per institution per year on its FY25 revenue                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Owner.com (US)                            | Restaurant and guest  | $249 a month plus 5%, or $499 flat; guests pay a 5% fee                                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| Cashfree UPI (2026)                       | Merchant or platform  | 1.95% plus 18% GST at list; 0% platform fee for new sign-ups until 31 March 2027, capped at ₹20 lakh of cumulative order value, with GST still charged on the waived fee. [Cashfree pricing](https://www.cashfree.com/docs/help/account/pricing)                  |
| Razorpay UPI (2026)                       | Merchant or platform  | 2% plus GST; 0% for new merchants up to ₹5 lakh or 90 days. [Razorpay pricing](https://razorpay.com/pricing/)                                                                                                                                                     |
| UPI MDR from 15 October 2026              | Merchant              | 0% up to ₹2,000; 0.4% above, capped at ₹300; small merchants (under ₹1 lakh a month on QR) stay at 0%; merchants may not pass it to customers. [SCC Online on the NPCI FAQ](https://www.scconline.com/blog/post/2026/09/16/npci-released-upi-mdr-faqs-explained/) |

Two conclusions fall straight out of this table. First, payments cannot be a revenue line for Juicy: there is no fee to share on a ₹200 UPI payment, and the gateway charges Juicy 2 to 2.3% unless on a short promotion. Second, 10% is above every pickup or direct-channel price in India and below only the aggregators, who bring new customers and riders for it.

### 5.2 The planned 10% commission, re-run with real numbers

On a ₹200 order at a 10% commission: gross commission ₹20. Gateway at list price (1.95% plus GST) ₹4.60. Split settlement for escrow about ₹0.47. Two WhatsApp status messages about ₹0.29. Net about ₹14.60 per order, or 7.3% of the order value. On Cashfree's promotion the net is about ₹18.50, but the ₹20 lakh cap equals 10,000 orders at ₹200: the roadmap's 10,000-orders-a-month scenario exhausts the promotion in its first month, and the 3,000-orders scenario in about three.

Monthly revenue after payment costs: 3,000 orders a month gives about ₹44,000; 10,000 orders gives about ₹1.46 lakh. The cheapest three-person operating team (a field executive at ₹30,000, a support executive at ₹22,000, a junior engineer at ₹40,000, plus ₹3,000 to 5,000 of hosting) costs about ₹95,000 to ₹100,000 a month before the founders pay themselves. So 10,000 prepaid orders a month on one campus roughly covers three junior salaries and nothing else; 3,000 covers one.

From the outlet's side: ₹20 out of a ₹200 order whose net profit is ₹10 to 24 at 5 to 12% margins. For a canteen on committee-fixed prices it is pure margin loss. For a café with free pricing it is a 10% price rise on app orders, which students will notice at the counter. Conclusion: the 10% commission is defensible only on genuinely new orders. On canteen-replacement orders it will be refused, or quietly worked around by staff telling regulars to pay cash at the counter.

### 5.3 The models, one by one

| Model                                                                     | Who pays                              | Realistic range (estimate)                                                                                                                                                | For                                                                                                                                                                         | Against                                                                                                                                                       | Does it scale?                                                     |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Monthly software fee per outlet                                           | Outlet or institution                 | ₹1,000 to 3,000 per outlet per month for a pickup board; ₹10,000 to 20,000 a year is the Petpooja-anchored ceiling for one counter                                        | Predictable; matches how outlets already buy software; no per-order arguments; survives low volume; keeps Juicy out of the money flow if the outlet collects payment itself | Needs a salesperson per sale; churn when the rush season ends (college holidays); small absolute revenue: 100 outlets at ₹2,000 is ₹2 lakh a month            | Linearly with sales effort                                         |
| Flat fee per order                                                        | Outlet                                | ₹2 to 5 per order (1 to 2.5% at ₹200); magicpin's ₹5 and Swiggy's 2023 ₹2 are the public anchors                                                                          | Simple, cheap enough to ignore, aligned with usage                                                                                                                          | Tiny until volume is large (10,000 orders × ₹3 = ₹30,000 a month); the gateway's 2.3% already eats most of ₹5 unless on promo                                 | Only with volume                                                   |
| Percentage commission                                                     | Outlet                                | 2 to 5% for walk-in-replacement orders (the DotPe, Thrive, ONDC band); 8 to 10% only where the platform also brings the site contract and management (the HungerBox band) | Scales with ticket size; no invoicing                                                                                                                                       | Exceeds net margin above about 5% unless orders are new; cannot be passed through where prices are committee-fixed; invites "pay cash at the counter" leakage | Yes, but at 2 to 5% the net per order after the gateway is ₹0 to 5 |
| Freemium                                                                  | Outlet                                | Free board plus a paid tier (analytics, deals, multi-store) at ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month                                                                                    | Removes friction where Zomato takeaway is free                                                                                                                              | Paid conversion in Indian small-business software is low; free users still cost gateway fees if money flows through Juicy                                     | Costly at scale                                                    |
| Premium plans for chains                                                  | Chains, branded cafés                 | ₹5,000 to 15,000 a month                                                                                                                                                  | Higher ticket; budgets exist                                                                                                                                                | Chains already have POS and CRM; Juicy has no integrations                                                                                                    | Few buyers                                                         |
| Customer subscription ("queue pass"); **ruled out by founder decision 2** | Student or employee                   | ₹29 to 99 a month (assumption; Swiggy says 70% of its users transact less than once a month because of affordability, which argues for the low end)                       | Direct consumer revenue; no restaurant objection                                                                                                                            | Students are the most price-sensitive group; the college may object to "pay to skip" fairness                                                                 | Per campus                                                         |
| Customer fee per order; **ruled out by founder decision 2**               | Student or employee                   | ₹2 to 5 per order; the aggregators charge ₹15 to 18                                                                                                                       | Market-proven; invisible next to aggregator fees; the one lever the market found money in                                                                                   | Fairness optics inside a canteen; must not be dressed as a UPI surcharge, which NPCI forbids; needs a lawyer's view                                           | Yes                                                                |
| Advertising                                                               | Brands, outlets                       | Nil until tens of thousands of daily users; Swiggy says ads "meaningfully kick in" only at tens of millions                                                               |                                                                                                                                                                             | No audience                                                                                                                                                   | Not for years                                                      |
| Loyalty and rewards                                                       | Outlet                                | ₹500 to 2,000 a month add-on                                                                                                                                              | Sticky                                                                                                                                                                      | Petpooja and Thrive already sell it                                                                                                                           | Add-on only                                                        |
| Payment revenue                                                           |                                       | Zero below ₹2,000; the gateway is a cost of 2 to 2.3%                                                                                                                     |                                                                                                                                                                             |                                                                                                                                                               |                                                                    |
| Institution-sponsored                                                     | College, park operator, corporate     | ₹50,000 to 3 lakh per campus per year (assumption anchored on SpaceBasic and HungerBox's management fees; no public rate)                                                 | The buyer who actually feels the queue pain and controls the tender; one contract covers all outlets                                                                        | Long sales cycles; procurement; must beat HungerBox and SmartQ bundles; college budgets are small                                                             | One contract per campus                                            |
| Hybrid                                                                    | Institution plus outlet plus customer | A flat campus fee plus ₹2 to 3 per order from the outlet (the customer-fee part is ruled out by founder decision 2)                                                       | Spreads a small cost over three parties who can each bear it                                                                                                                | Three invoices                                                                                                                                                | The most plausible of the set                                      |

### 5.4 What the five models earn per outlet (illustrative arithmetic)

Using the base-case volume from the financial model in section 13 (500 orders a month at an average ₹180, so ₹90,000 of food a month through one outlet), and the cost inputs from section 9:

| Model                                                                                                  | Revenue per outlet per month | Direct costs | Gross profit per outlet per month | Gross profit per order |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------- | ------------ | --------------------------------- | ---------------------- |
| A. Commission only, 8%                                                                                 | ₹7,200                       | ₹3,585       | ₹3,615                            | ₹7.2                   |
| B. Flat fee ₹1,499 a month; the outlet collects payment itself, so Juicy pays no gateway               | ₹1,499                       | ₹1,245       | ₹254                              | ₹0.5                   |
| C. Hybrid ₹499 a month plus 3%                                                                         | ₹3,199                       | ₹3,585       | loss of ₹386                      | loss of ₹0.8           |
| D. Customer-paid queue-skip fee ₹5 an order, nothing from the outlet (ruled out by founder decision 2) | ₹2,500                       | ₹3,585       | loss of ₹1,085                    | loss of ₹2.2           |
| E. Commission 8% plus a ₹5 customer fee (ruled out by founder decision 2)                              | ₹9,700                       | ₹3,585       | ₹6,115                            | ₹12.2                  |

Read this carefully, because it is the heart of the business-model problem. If Juicy is in the money flow (models A, C, D, E), the gateway and support costs are about ₹3,585 a month per outlet at this volume, so a low commission or a small fee alone cannot cover them. If Juicy stays out of the money flow (model B), its costs collapse but so does its revenue, and ₹1,499 a month only works if support is nearly free. The only models that make money per outlet are the ones outlets and students have the clearest reasons to refuse (A and E). Volume is the variable that changes everything: at 2,000 orders a month per outlet, model C earns about ₹6,000 of gross profit and model D about ₹1,000. That is why the pilot must measure orders per outlet before anyone chooses a price.

### 5.5 What this implies about the current plan

- The plan's single lever (a commission, worked at 10%) has the clearest evidence against it in the first market. Of the alternatives, a flat outlet subscription has market precedent. A small customer fee does too, but founder decision 2 rules it out, so the outlet and the institution must carry the whole price.
- "The platform pays the gateway" holds only on a promotion. At list rates the gateway takes about a quarter of a 10% commission and all of a 2% one.
- If Juicy must add 5% GST to orders from a canteen that charges none at its counter, either the student pays ₹210 instead of ₹200 or the canteen absorbs it, which makes a 10% commission cost the canteen 15% (the independent pass's "GST wedge", Part C).
- Escrow until pickup is a cost (a payment-aggregator contract, float, refunds) with no revenue, and it settles slower than Zomato's daily payout for small outlets. It is a customer-trust feature, not a selling point to the outlet.
- A pilot that keeps Juicy out of the money flow (the outlet's own UPI QR, or pay at the counter) avoids the aggregator contract, the escrow design and the RBI questions in section 8, and may reduce the GST exposure (a chartered accountant must confirm), at the cost of the commission model. Given the evidence, that is a trade worth making for the first six months. It also sidesteps a hurdle the independent pass found: Razorpay's split-settlement product now requires ₹40 lakh of prior turnover (Part C).

## 6. Competitors, and the gap Juicy could occupy

### 6.1 The short version

1. **Pickup is not a new idea in India, and both giants built it.** Zomato Pickup, October 2018, 13 cities, 20,000 restaurants. Swiggy Takeaway, 50 days later, Bengaluru first. In November 2020 Zomato made takeaway zero-commission for 55,000 restaurants and still only did "tens of thousands" of takeaway orders a week against roughly 3.8 million delivery orders a week, about 0.5 to 2.5% of volume. Neither company has mentioned the feature in two years of investor material.
2. **The Bengaluru campuses with money are already contracted.** HungerBox (Bengaluru; 891 cafeterias, 13.23 million orders a month by its own count; FY24 revenue ₹65.8 crore), SmartQ (Compass Group; lists IIM Bangalore), GoKhana (200,000 orders a day by its own count) and SpaceBasic (Bengaluru; PES, Reva, Manipal, NITTE as named clients). Their product is Juicy's roadmap: pre-order, token, wallet or UPI, kitchen screen, multi-vendor dashboard. They sell to the institution, not to the canteen.
3. **The closest clones are tiny and unfunded.** MealPe (Ahmedabad, founded March 2023, one undisclosed angel round, "commission-free"), SkipQ (Ghaziabad, three student founders) and HungerTap are direct "college canteen, skip the queue" clones. None discloses campuses live, orders or revenue.
4. **Juicy's planned 10% commission sits in an awkward middle.** Aggregators take about 23 to 25% of order value in total but bring new customers and do the delivery. Direct-ordering tools charge 1 to 3% (DotPe, Thrive) or a flat ₹999 to ₹12,000 a month (SlickPOS, Petpooja). MealPe says "commission-free". magicpin spent ₹100 crore telling restaurants "zero commission". Captive-cafeteria platforms appear to earn only a few rupees per order. A canteen owner is being asked to pay ₹20 on a ₹200 order from a student who was already standing in his building.
5. **The gap that remains is real but narrow:** independent canteens, food courts and cafés on and around campuses that no institution-wide contract covers, where the operator is too small for HungerBox or SmartQ and the giants' delivery model does not reach the counter.

### 6.2 The competitive map

| Category                                 | India                                                                                              | Global (for lessons)                                                                | Overlap with Juicy                                                       |
| ---------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Delivery aggregators with pickup options | Zomato (Eternal), Swiggy, magicpin on ONDC                                                         | Grubhub, DoorDash                                                                   | Customer habit, restaurant relationships, a pickup feature already built |
| Captive-cafeteria and campus platforms   | HungerBox, SmartQ (Compass), GoKhana, SpaceBasic, MealPe, SkipQ, HungerTap                         | Tapingo (now Grubhub Campus), Transact and CBORD (now Illumia), Snackpass, Starship | Direct: same user, same counter, same features                           |
| Restaurant direct-ordering software      | DotPe (with Rista POS), Thrive, Petpooja Online Ordering, UrbanPiper Meraki, LimeTray, Restroworks | Owner.com, Olo, Toast, Square Online, ChowNow, Slice, Flipdish, QikServe            | The "own channel, low commission" pitch                                  |
| QR and table ordering                    | DotPe, Petpooja QR, Restroworks RestroSCAN                                                         | me&u                                                                                | Adjacent (dine-in, not pickup)                                           |
| POS (billing software)                   | Petpooja, Restroworks, Rista, SlickPOS, LimeTray                                                   | Toast, Square                                                                       | Every serious restaurant has one; Juicy must integrate or be ignored     |
| Reservations, discovery, dining deals    | EazyDiner, District (Eternal), Swiggy Dineout, magicpin                                            |                                                                                     | Not pickup; competes for the restaurant's marketing budget               |
| Corporate meal programmes                | Zomato for Enterprise, HungerBox, SmartQ                                                           | Ritual for Companies                                                                | Office pickup is a possible second market                                |
| Surplus food                             | None found in India                                                                                | Too Good To Go                                                                      | Juicy's "surplus deals" idea                                             |

### 6.3 The players that matter most

**Zomato (Eternal).** Food delivery order value ₹10,769 crore in the quarter to June 2025; the platform's total take across both sides was about 24.7% of gross order value (adjusted revenue ₹2,657 crore divided by order value; this includes customer-paid fees, so the restaurant's commission alone is lower). [Eternal shareholders' letter Q1 FY26, Jul 2025](https://b.zmtcdn.com/investor-relations/Eternal_Shareholders_Letter_Q1FY26_Results.pdf) Industry guides put the restaurant-side commission at 18 to 25% base and 25 to 35% effective after platform fees, gateway fees and GST on fees. [DineOpen commission guide, 2026](https://www.dineopen.com/blog/swiggy-zomato-commission-calculator-guide.html) It has launched and withdrawn Zomato Instant, Zomato Everyday and Zomato Quick (15-minute delivery, pulled after four months in May 2025). [Restaurant India, May 2025](https://www.restaurantindia.in/news/zomato-ends-15-minute-food-delivery-on-main-app-shifts-focus-to-blinkit.n12723) Its CEO wrote in July 2025: "we do not see any innovation in the space which makes us believe that this business is under any obvious threat." Zomato for Enterprise (August 2024) is delivery billed to employers, not canteen ordering. Why restaurants use it: demand they cannot generate themselves. Why they resent it: the 25 to 35% deduction and forced discounts.

**Swiggy.** Food delivery order value ₹9,490 crore in the quarter to June 2026, 19.2 million monthly users, total take about 23.3%. [IIFL, Jul 2026](https://www.indiainfoline.com/news/earnings/swiggy-share-price-slip-3-after-q1-fy27-results-revenue-jumps-37-net-loss-narrows) It launches and kills formats fast: Genie shut May 2025; SNACC, a standalone 15-minute snack-delivery app, ran January 2025 to February 2026 and was shut because small snack baskets could not produce sustainable margins. [Medianama, Feb 2026](https://www.medianama.com/2026/02/223-swiggy-snacc-shutdown-15-minute-delivery/) That basket (snacks, coffee, ₹100 to ₹200) is Juicy's basket. In October 2022, more than 900 restaurants walked out of Swiggy Dineout over deep discounting and 4 to 10% commissions. [Inc42, Oct 2022](https://inc42.com/buzz/900-restaurants-opt-out-of-swiggy-dineout-amid-deep-discounting-issues/) Lesson: restaurants tolerate a small commission only if the platform never forces discounts out of their margin.

**magicpin and ONDC.** The largest food seller on the government-backed open network: 1.5 lakh daily food and logistics orders and 70,000 restaurants as of October 2024, with a ₹100 crore programme offering zero commission, zero onboarding fees and free delivery to new restaurants. [Inc42, Oct 2024](https://inc42.com/buzz/magicpin-clocks-1-5-lakh-daily-food-logistics-orders-on-ondc/) No pickup feature was found. The point: "zero commission" is already a phrase Bengaluru restaurant owners have heard.

**HungerBox (Bengaluru).** The institution is the client. It handles site setup, vendor deployment and operations; pricing is unpublished. Its college product is literally "students order before their break, collect without queuing", with token tracking, UPI and meal cards, and named education clients GITAM, ICFAI and Oberoi International. [HungerBox colleges](https://www.hungerbox.ai/college-cafeteria-management/) It raised $43.67 million (last round August 2020) and made ₹65.8 crore in FY24. [Inc42 profile](https://inc42.com/company/hungerbox/) Dividing FY24 revenue by current annual orders gives roughly ₹4 of revenue per order (a rough estimate; the periods do not match). Juicy's model assumes ₹20 per order.

**SmartQ (Compass Group).** Founded in Bengaluru in 2015, raised only $2.24 million, and was acquired by Compass on 18 March 2021. [Tracxn](https://tracxn.com/d/companies/smartq/__aEVg7E82IyHs-SW_BC6qPCPCrvFi7YDP7P6cxSwFAB4) Its university page promises "seamless ordering and pickup", real-time pickup notifications and hostel delivery, and names IIM Bangalore, Thapar, LPU, O.P. Jindal and others. [SmartQ universities](https://www.thesmartq.com/university) It bundles food operations with the technology, which a pure app cannot.

**SpaceBasic (Bengaluru).** Campus-operations software with a digital-cafeteria module: student wallet with UPI top-up, pre-ordering with kitchen production targets, biometric or ID verification at collection. Named clients PES, Manipal, Reva, DY Patil, NITTE. Priced "per student per year". FY25 revenue was ₹2.2 crore across 100 or more institutions, which is at most ₹2.2 lakh per institution per year. [SpaceBasic](https://www.spacebasic.com/); [Inc42 profile](https://inc42.com/company/spacebasic/) Campus software in India is a small-ticket business.

**MealPe (Ahmedabad), the closest clone.** Founded 14 March 2023. "Browse menus, pre-order meals, and skip the queue"; "Scan. Order. Collect. No Queues."; a "commission-free pre-order system". One undisclosed angel round (October 2023); it claimed expressions of interest from 50 or more campuses in 2023; its site shows "lakh+ meals processed" with the exact figures obscured, no named clients and no revenue. [MealPe](https://mealpe.app/office-cafeteria-university-canteen-pre-order-system/); [Crunchbase](https://www.crunchbase.com/organization/mealpe) Three years in with no visible growth is itself a data point about the segment.

**DotPe (Gurugram).** Started as QR and WhatsApp ordering to bypass aggregator commissions; now POS, online store, payments. Raised $93.5 million (Temasek, PayU, Google among investors); FY25 revenue ₹82.8 crore. [Inc42 profile](https://inc42.com/company/dotpe/) Competitor blogs describe a free QR tier and 1 to 3% commission per online order. A customer quote on its site: "we've been able to reduce our 3rd party commission by a whopping 98%". [DotPe](https://dotpe.in/)

**Thrive (Hashtag Loyalty).** Branded direct-ordering site for restaurants: "just 3% per order for delivery and takeaway", commission-free dine-in, free setup in 15 minutes; 9,500 restaurants in 76 cities and ₹30 crore or more of orders fulfilled. [Razorpay app store listing](https://razorpay.com/app-store/thrive/) That cumulative order value is about ₹31,600 per restaurant in total: direct channels stay small unless the restaurant itself markets them.

**Petpooja (Ahmedabad), the incumbent at the counter.** 150,000 or more businesses; its own ordering website, QR scan-and-order, and integrations with Swiggy, Zomato and 150 third parties; it says about 25% of all Swiggy and Zomato orders flow through its POS. [Petpooja online ordering](https://www.petpooja.com/poss/online-order-management-software) A competitor's pricing guide (March 2026) estimates ₹3,000 to ₹12,000 a month per outlet, with online ordering as a ₹1,500 to ₹3,000 add-on and hardware ₹15,000 to ₹50,000. [DineOpen Petpooja pricing, Mar 2026](https://www.dineopen.com/blog/petpooja-pricing-plans-2026.html) A canteen that already runs Petpooja can switch on a QR channel for a few thousand rupees a month and pay no per-order cut.

**UrbanPiper (Bengaluru).** The plumbing between restaurants and ordering channels; 40,000 restaurants; Swiggy and Zomato are both investors. [Inc42](https://inc42.com/buzz/zomato-backed-saas-startup-urbanpiper-bags-24-mn-funding-led-by-sequoia-capital-tiger-global/) A Juicy that scales to chains would have to appear as a channel inside UrbanPiper or Petpooja, or chains will not touch it.

**Others, briefly.** Restroworks (formerly Posist; 25,000 restaurants; enterprise chains) and LimeTray (4,500 restaurants; online ordering at roughly $35 to $69 a month) sell to chains that would never pay 10% per order for pickup. EazyDiner is reservations and bill payment, not pickup. SlickPOS lists a Smart plan at ₹999 a month: the market price of "a POS with QR ordering" is about ₹1,000 a month. No restaurant order-ahead tool from Paytm or PhonePe was verified.

### 6.4 What the global players teach

| Player                                                       | What happened                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Lesson                                                                                                                                 |
| ------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Tapingo (US campuses)                                        | The exact Juicy idea, launched 2012, paid with meal plans; 200 or more campuses; acquired by Grubhub in September 2018 for about $150 million; the standalone app was shut in 2019 and folded into Grubhub Campus Dining. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tapingo) | The idea can build a company, but its asset was dining-operator contracts and meal-plan integration, and the exit was to an aggregator |
| Transact and CBORD (now Illumia)                             | The campus-card and meal-plan owners control ordering in the US. [Illumia](https://illumiatech.com/)                                                                                                                                                                         | In India that role is empty at most colleges: an opening and a warning (SpaceBasic is trying to fill it)                               |
| Snackpass                                                    | Began as campus order-ahead with social gifting; its homepage now leads with creator marketing plus ordering and POS. [Snackpass](https://www.snackpass.co/)                                                                                                                 | Even a well-funded campus pickup app pivoted towards marketing and POS to find revenue                                                 |
| Ritual                                                       | Order-ahead for office workers; sells to companies and buildings. [Ritual](https://ritual.co/)                                                                                                                                                                               | Office districts are the natural second market after campuses                                                                          |
| Allset                                                       | Pre-order pickup with cashback; now part of SoundHound AI. [Allset](https://allsetnow.com/)                                                                                                                                                                                  | Consumer order-ahead apps tend to end as acquisitions                                                                                  |
| Owner.com                                                    | Branded site and app for independents: $249 a month plus 5% per order, or $499 flat; guests pay a 5% "order support fee". [Owner.com pricing](https://www.owner.com/pricing)                                                                                                 | The US benchmark: about 5% or a flat fee, with the consumer carrying a visible fee. Juicy plans no customer fee                        |
| Olo, Toast, Square, ChowNow, Slice, Flipdish, QikServe, me&u | Ordering became a feature inside POS and payments; direct ordering is sold at a flat fee or near-zero commission (ChowNow: "unlimited commission-free orders"; Flipdish €99 to €129 a month)                                                                                 | Money in restaurant tech is in payments and POS, not the ordering screen                                                               |
| Too Good To Go                                               | Surplus "surprise bags" at a third of the price; 164,000 partners, 62 million users, a small profit in 2024; not in India. [Wikipedia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Too_Good_To_Go)                                                                                         | Juicy's surplus-deals idea has a proven template and no Indian precedent                                                               |

Pattern: every standalone order-ahead app was acquired, pivoted, or survives as a feature inside a bigger system. None stayed an independent 10%-commission marketplace.

### 6.5 Did Zomato and Swiggy already try this, and what happened?

Yes, and the numbers are the most important fact in this section. Takeaway on an aggregator, after two years in the app and with zero commission, reached roughly 0.5 to 2.5% of orders (estimate from Zomato's own November 2020 statement of "tens of thousands" of takeaway orders a week against about 3.8 million delivery orders a week). The giants have proved three things at once: they can ship pickup in weeks; they will give it away at 0% when it suits them; and their users do not pick it in volume, because the Zomato and Swiggy habit is "food comes to me". That is simultaneously the best evidence that the giants will not prioritise Juicy's niche, and the best evidence that pickup demand, when offered inside a general app, is small.

### 6.6 The exact gap

Prepaid, counter-native pickup for independent canteens, cafés and food courts on and around campuses that no institution-wide contract covers, where:

1. the aggregators' delivery model does not reach the counter (no rider economics inside a campus, and ₹100 to ₹200 baskets that SNACC showed cannot carry delivery costs);
2. HungerBox, SmartQ and GoKhana will not sell a managed-services contract to a single canteen;
3. the canteen's existing POS can show a QR menu but does not solve the collection problem: calling tokens, proving the order is yours, holding the money until pickup, handling no-shows, and reselling uncollected prepaid food.

To deserve to exist, Juicy must be different from all three groups at once:

- **Against aggregators:** be the campus's operating layer (tokens, estimated time, stock, pause, counter board), not a marketplace. Nothing suggests Zomato or Swiggy want the counter.
- **Against cafeteria operators:** be self-serve and cheap enough that a canteen contractor signs up in an afternoon without the college's procurement process. MealPe and HungerTap claim this but have not shown it working.
- **Against POS and QR tools:** own the three things a QR menu does not: prepayment with refund rules, a no-show policy, and surplus resale. These are workflow and money-handling, which is where the roadmap already points.

**Pricing has to change.** Ten percent on a pickup order is above every direct-channel benchmark in India (DotPe 1 to 3%, Thrive 3%, MealPe 0%, magicpin 0% on ONDC) and roughly five times what captive-cafeteria platforms appear to earn per order. A defensible structure is closer to Owner.com's: a small flat monthly fee plus a low per-order fee (2 to 4%), or a per-order fee the customer visibly pays for the queue-skip (₹5 to ₹10), which no Indian player charges today. Juicy's "no customer-side fee" assumption should be tested, not assumed.

### 6.7 Four direct answers

**Why would a restaurant choose Juicy over the alternatives?** Only if it visibly raises throughput at peak (more orders served per break, fewer abandoned queues) and the fee is small enough to be paid out of that uplift. That is an operations sale with a measurable before-and-after, not a "lower commission than Zomato" sale, because the orders Juicy brings come mostly from students who were already in the building.

**Why would a customer use Juicy instead of Zomato or Swiggy?** Only where the canteen itself pushes it: tokens called from Juicy, a dedicated pickup window, and real minutes saved at peak. Without that in-venue enforcement the app will sit unused next to Zomato, exactly as takeaway did inside the aggregators.

**How likely and how fast would competitors copy it?** The giants can copy it in weeks (Swiggy's 50-day follow in 2018) but have twice built and sidelined pickup, and their current experiments are all delivery. Cafeteria operators already have the product; the question is whether they chase small canteens, which their managed-services model is not built for. Petpooja or DotPe could add "pickup token plus prepaid" in one release cycle and have the distribution. Clones already exist; their constraint is distribution, not code. The app is copyable in weeks by anyone; campus contracts and counter operations are what take time.

**What would make Juicy hard to copy?** Honestly, very little in the software. By analogy with Tapingo (dining contracts and meal-plan integration were what Grubhub paid for) and SmartQ and SpaceBasic (institution contracts): exclusive or default status on a campus; density inside one campus (a dozen counters on one app with one wallet); the regulated, fiddly money-handling rules (escrow until pickup, refunds when the restaurant is at fault, paid on no-show, surplus resale); and low-end-device performance, which is a real advantage for this user but a feature, not a moat.

### 6.8 Added after the independent pass: the consumer-side price war

Pass 1 focused on the business-to-business competition above. The independent pass (Part C) found a second front that matters just as much for students, because they are price-led:

| Player                     | What it is                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Why it matters to Juicy                                                                                                          |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Swiggy Toing               | A separate budget app: "zero platform fees, no packaging charges, prices that match or beat dine-in menus", aimed at "Gen Z, college students and first-time earners", launched in "key education hubs" and in Bengaluru since March 2026. [Fortune India, Feb 2026](https://www.fortuneindia.com/business-news/swiggys-toing-enters-delhi-ncr-bets-on-zero-fee-model-to-disrupt-food-delivery-market/130700) A CLSA estimate puts it at about 33 million weekly active users, 96% of Zomato's, within a year ([Moneycontrol, Sep 2026](https://www.moneycontrol.com/news/business/startup/swiggy-s-toing-user-base-now-almost-as-large-as-zomato-s-clsa-14025987.html); app-usage estimates, not orders, and not re-checked for this report). Eternal calls its impact "limited" and "purely price-driven". | Students in Juicy's city now have a no-fee app whose prices match or beat the counter. Juicy can never be dearer than walking up |
| Swiggy Deskeats            | An office-meal proposition in 7,000 or more tech parks across 30 cities. [Swiggy Q2 FY26 letter](https://www.swiggy.com/corporate/wp-content/uploads/2025/10/Q2-FY2026-Shareholder-letter.pdf)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | The office half of the "skip the queue" audience is already served                                                               |
| Rapido Ownly               | Zero commission, menu prices at offline levels; now about 25,000 Bengaluru restaurant partners and about 50,000 orders a day, expanding to all metros from October to December 2026. [Mint, 26 Sep 2026](https://www.livemint.com/companies/news/why-your-100-meal-costs-250-online-and-how-rapido-wants-to-change-that-11790418590126.html)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Bengaluru restaurants already have a zero-commission option that also delivers                                                   |
| Flipkart "Eat In"          | Food delivery being tested with employees in Bengaluru, reportedly targeting a 10% commission. [Entrackr, Sep 2026](https://entrackr.com/news/flipkart-starts-employee-testing-of-eat-in-food-delivery-service-in-bengaluru-12510648)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | A fourth deep-pocketed player in Juicy's home city                                                                               |
| ONDC food                  | In "steady decline through 2025" after incentives were cut. [Financial Express, Jul 2026](https://www.financialexpress.com/business/news/flipkart-picks-standalone-route-for-food-delivery/4286636/)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         | Subsidised low-commission networks do not hold restaurants once the subsidy stops                                                |
| Grubhub Campus Dining (US) | DoorDash agreed on 15 September 2026 to buy it for $300 million; live at more than 450 US colleges; closing expected by mid-2027. Checked on [DoorDash's investor-relations page](https://ir.doordash.com/news/news-details/2026/DoorDash-and-Wonder-Announce-Strategic-Partnership/default.aspx)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | The best evidence that campus ordering has real value, as infrastructure sold to institutions, bought by a delivery giant        |

The combined picture: Juicy is squeezed from above (no-fee consumer apps with counter-matching prices) and from beside (institutional cafeteria platforms). What neither group offers is counter-side pickup on a campus they do not already contract: that remains the opening, and it remains small.

### 6.9 What could not be verified

Whether Zomato Takeaway or Swiggy Takeaway is live in the Bengaluru apps today; Swiggy One and Dineout counts; ONDC food volumes for 2025-26 and whether ONDC supports self-pickup; HungerBox, SmartQ and GoKhana commercial terms; MealPe's live campuses; Snackpass funding; Hangry; Boost. (Grubhub Campus Dining's current scale was later confirmed; see 6.8.) Silence on these is not evidence either way.

## 7. Market size: how big is this, honestly?

### 7.1 The facts the estimate is built on

| Input                                        | Value                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Status                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| -------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| India food-services market                   | ₹5.69 lakh crore in FY24, growing 8.1% a year to ₹7.77 lakh crore by 2028; organised share 43.9% rising to 52.9%                                                                                                                                     | Fact. [NRAI IFSR 2024 via Business Standard](https://www.business-standard.com/industry/news/indian-food-services-sector-to-grow-by-8-1-from-2024-to-2028-report-124070900997_1.html)                                       |
| Redseer's view                               | About $90 billion in FY26, $150 billion by 2030; online delivery is 11% now, 18% by FY31; 3 crore monthly online users, up from 1 crore in FY21                                                                                                      | Fact. [Redseer, Sep 2026](https://redseer.com/digests/india-food-services-market-growth-profitability-2030/)                                                                                                                |
| Online food delivery, the two listed players | Swiggy FY26: 714 million orders, ₹34,593 crore of order value, average order ₹484, take rate 25.6%, contribution ₹36.5 per order, operating profit ₹14 per order. Eternal Q1 FY27: ₹10,769 crore net order value, 27.2 million monthly customers     | Fact. [Swiggy KPI book](https://www.swiggy.com/corporate/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/KPI-Book_Q1FY27.xlsx); [Eternal letter](https://b.zmtcdn.com/investor-relations/Eternal_Limited_Shareholders_Letter_Q1FY27_Results.pdf) |
| Combined delivery market                     | About ₹80,000 to 85,000 crore of order value a year and 1.7 to 1.8 billion orders (estimate; matches Redseer's 11% of $90 billion)                                                                                                                   | Estimate                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Number of restaurants in India               | No clean public figure. NRAI "represents over 500,000"; Mumbai alone has 141,456 by NRAI's count; Eternal has 328,000 active partners                                                                                                                | Fact for each piece; the total is not known                                                                                                                                                                                 |
| Branded coffee shops                         | 5,339 outlets in 2025, forecast more than 6,100 in 2026; Tata Starbucks 502                                                                                                                                                                          | Fact (trade press)                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Higher education                             | 46,468 colleges, 1,278 universities, 11,787 stand-alone institutions, about 4.5 crore students                                                                                                                                                       | Fact. [AISHE 2023-24](https://www.dohe-education.gov.in/static/uploads/2026/07/8616f33dfee644ab6b87a2bd0658b18d.pdf)                                                                                                        |
| Corporate cafeterias                         | HungerBox ran 550 cafeterias for 126 corporates in 2020; FY25 ₹885.9 crore of transactions                                                                                                                                                           | Fact; the national count is unknown                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Malls and food courts                        | No reliable national count found                                                                                                                                                                                                                     | Gap                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| Restaurant software                          | Petpooja about 1 lakh restaurants (its own and press figures range from 45,000 to 1.5 lakh); three vendors hold about 85% of organised POS; 652 restaurant-software startups tracked, total funding ever $66.7 million, $0.5 million in 2026 to date | Fact. [Tracxn](https://tracxn.com/d/explore/restaurant-saas-startups-in-india/__aEoQyNayDOyz6h41CEe6tPRpjv1YtPV-0geHSnIPaK4)                                                                                                |

There is no Indian statistic for pre-ordered pickup. Swiggy and Eternal do not disclose a pickup share, NRAI does not break it out, and Redseer has no figure. So everything below is built from outlet counts and people counts with stated assumptions. Treat the ranges, not the midpoints, as the finding.

### 7.2 Supply side: outlets × money per outlet

How much could one outlet be worth to the platform in a year?

| Per outlet, per year                                                                                   | Conservative | Base       | Aggressive |
| ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------ | ---------- | ---------- |
| Walk-in transactions a day (assumption)                                                                | 150          | 250        | 400        |
| Operating days (assumption)                                                                            | 300          | 300        | 300        |
| Share pre-ordered through Juicy (assumption; Starbucks in the US reached about 3 in 10 after a decade) | 4%           | 10%        | 25%        |
| Average order (assumption; canteens are cheaper than the ₹200 café figure)                             | ₹120         | ₹150       | ₹200       |
| Pickup order value through Juicy                                                                       | ₹2.2 lakh    | ₹11.3 lakh | ₹60 lakh   |
| Platform revenue at a 10% commission                                                                   | ₹21,600      | ₹1.1 lakh  | ₹6 lakh    |
| Platform revenue at a flat fee (₹1,000 / ₹2,000 / ₹3,000 a month)                                      | ₹12,000      | ₹24,000    | ₹36,000    |

How many outlets are even candidates (places where ordering ahead to skip a queue is plausible):

| Candidate outlets, India                                        | Count                                    | Status                                                                   |
| --------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Campus food outlets: about 59,500 institutions × 1 to 2 outlets | 60,000 to 120,000                        | Estimate on a fact base; most colleges are small with one canteen if any |
| Branded cafés                                                   | About 6,100                              | Fact (secondary)                                                         |
| Quick-prep restaurant counters (Swiggy Bolt-type)               | About 40,000                             | Secondary, not re-verified                                               |
| Office cafeterias and tech-park food courts                     | Unknown; HungerBox alone ran 550 in 2020 | Assumption 5,000 to 15,000                                               |
| Total                                                           | About 110,000 to 180,000                 | Estimate                                                                 |

India TAM in platform revenue (outlets × revenue per outlet):

| Case         | Outlets                                | Commission model                               | Flat-fee model    |
| ------------ | -------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------- | ----------------- |
| Conservative | 60,000 (campuses only, low throughput) | ₹130 crore a year                              | ₹72 crore a year  |
| Base         | 110,000                                | ₹1,210 crore a year                            | ₹264 crore a year |
| Aggressive   | 180,000                                | ₹10,800 crore a year (not credible, see below) | ₹648 crore a year |

The sanity check that kills the aggressive case: Swiggy's entire food-delivery revenue in FY26 was ₹8,867 crore on 714 million delivered orders.
