| Nice-to-have     | Order again, favourites, "your usuals"                                                                                                                  | Helpful for retention, not for the first test.                                                                                                                                                         |
| Nice-to-have     | Ratings per dish                                                                                                                                        | Useful later; at one canteen the owner already knows.                                                                                                                                                  |
| Nice-to-have     | Surplus deals (re-selling uncollected food cheaply)                                                                                                     | Clever, but it needs volume and prepayment first.                                                                                                                                                      |
| Do NOT build yet | Multi-brand, multi-branch catalog hierarchy; memberships and store roles                                                                                | Needed at restaurant number 20, not number 1.                                                                                                                                                          |
| Do NOT build yet | Escrow ledger, deferred splits, reconciliation service                                                                                                  | Simplest money model first (the outlet collects, or the aggregator pays the outlet daily).                                                                                                             |
| Do NOT build yet | ETA engine, QR pickup verification, recommender, analytics pipeline, cost-of-goods tracking                                                             | Each is a month of work that tests nothing about demand.                                                                                                                                               |
| Do NOT build yet | AWS migration, Kubernetes, load testing to 1,000 concurrent users                                                                                       | Free tiers are fine until a real campus shows up in the numbers.                                                                                                                                       |
| Do NOT build yet | Native app store apps (founder decision 1: website first; a store app later only if a pilot shows a measured need, such as "ready" alerts not arriving) | The web app installs to the home screen; an app store listing adds cost and friction.                                                                                                                  |

### The "aha" moments

- Customer: the first time they walk past a 15-person queue, say their number, and pick up food that is already packed. It has to happen on the first or second order, at peak, with no mistake. The second aha is the first "ready" buzz arriving while they are still in class.
- Outlet owner or staff: the first rush where the board shows the orders in sequence, nobody shouts, and the counter clears faster. For the owner the aha is the end-of-day screen showing "₹X came in before anyone reached the counter" and the first regular who now orders every day without being asked.

### Numbers that would show product-market fit (targets are assumptions to be revised after the pilot)

| Metric                                                         | What it tells you                          | Target before calling it "fit"                                                                             |
| -------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Share of the outlet's peak-hour orders that come through Juicy | Does the product matter where the pain is  | 15% by week 4 at the pilot outlet, 30% by month 3                                                          |
| Repeat purchase rate                                           | Did the first order feel good              | 50% of first-time orderers order again within 14 days                                                      |
| Weekly active customers ÷ customers who ever ordered           | Habit or novelty                           | Above 35% after month 2                                                                                    |
| Orders per active customer per week                            | Is it a daily habit or an occasional trick | 2 or more                                                                                                  |
| Customer retention at day 30 (cohort)                          | Do people stay                             | 25% or more of a signup cohort still ordering a month later                                                |
| Restaurant activation                                          | Did the outlet actually switch it on       | 70% of signed outlets process 20 or more orders in their first week                                        |
| Restaurant retention                                           | Would they miss it                         | 80% of pilot outlets still live after 3 months                                                             |
| Revenue per outlet per month                                   | Is there a business                        | More than the outlet pays for a cleaner's day: ₹2,000 or more per outlet per month from fees or commission |
| Outlet churn                                                   | Is the tool sticky                         | Under 5% of outlets a month                                                                                |
| No-show rate on prepaid orders                                 | Is prepayment working                      | Under 3% of orders                                                                                         |
| NPS (customer)                                                 | Would they recommend it                    | 40 or more                                                                                                 |
| "Very disappointed if it went away" (the Sean Ellis question)  | The classic fit test                       | 40% or more of active users                                                                                |
| Referral share                                                 | Are students bringing friends              | 20% or more of new users arrive through another user                                                       |
| Order accuracy and on-time rate                                | Is the promise kept                        | 97% of orders ready within the promised window                                                             |

The independent pass's base expectation (Part C) is that 3 to 6% of a campus's students will still order weekly three months after launch without discounts. Part D shows why, on a single busy counter, that is roughly the same bar as "15% of peak orders" above, so track both.

## 13. A three-year illustrative model: conservative, base, aggressive

These are not forecasts. They are three internally consistent stories, computed from the assumptions printed under each table, so that the founder can see which assumptions matter. Change any input and the whole table moves. All amounts in ₹ lakh (1 lakh = 100,000; 100 lakh = 1 crore).

Shared assumptions: payment gateway 1.5% of order value in year 1 (the capped 0% promotion blended with list price) and 2.3% in years 2 and 3; split settlement 0.3%; refund losses 0.3%; messages ₹0.30 per order; hosting ₹5,000 a month plus ₹0.15 per order; support ₹1.50 per order; one field sales executive at ₹38,000 a month signs 5 outlets a month; onboarding ₹3,000 per outlet; ₹40 per new customer; 3 orders per active customer a month; the two founders pay themselves ₹50,000 each from month 7; an engineer costs ₹70,000 a month; ₹10,000 a month of base marketing; every new outlet ramps from 20% to 100% of its steady-state orders over six months.

### Conservative: a few cafés, slow ramp, 7% commission

New outlets a month: 2 in year 1, 3 in year 2, 5 in year 3. Outlet churn 6% a month. 250 orders per outlet a month at ₹150. One engineer hired in month 13.

| Year | Outlets (end) | Active customers (end) | Orders  | Order value | Revenue | Direct costs | Gross margin | Sales | Marketing | Salaries | Operating result | Cumulative |
| ---- | ------------- | ---------------------- | ------- | ----------- | ------- | ------------ | ------------ | ----- | --------- | -------- | ---------------- | ---------- |
| 1    | 17            | 1,033                  | 18,305  | 27.5        | 1.9     | 1.5          | 20%          | 5.3   | 2.1       | 6.0      | -12.9            | -12.9      |
| 2    | 35            | 2,242                  | 61,372  | 92.1        | 6.4     | 4.5          | 31%          | 5.6   | 4.1       | 20.4     | -28.1            | -41.1      |
| 3    | 60            | 3,952                  | 113,700 | 170.6       | 11.9    | 7.8          | 35%          | 6.4   | 6.5       | 20.4     | -29.1            | -70.2      |

Per outlet: gross profit about ₹1,050 a month; LTV ₹17,500; LTV ÷ CAC 1.7; payback 10 months. No break-even within 36 months. This is what happens if the pilot shows only modest usage and the team keeps going anyway: ₹70 lakh of losses for a 60-outlet business.

### Base: steady campus-by-campus growth, 8% commission

New outlets a month: 3, then 6, then 10. Churn 4%. 500 orders per outlet a month at ₹180. One engineer from month 7, two from month 19.

| Year | Outlets (end) | Active customers (end) | Orders  | Order value | Revenue | Direct costs | Gross margin | Sales | Marketing | Salaries | Operating result | Cumulative |
| ---- | ------------- | ---------------------- | ------- | ----------- | ------- | ------------ | ------------ | ----- | --------- | -------- | ---------------- | ---------- |
| 1    | 29            | 3,531                  | 60,469  | 108.8       | 8.7     | 4.1          | 53%          | 5.6   | 4.0       | 10.2     | -15.2            | -15.2      |
| 2    | 76            | 10,028                 | 249,731 | 449.5       | 36.0    | 18.5         | 49%          | 11.3  | 12.9      | 24.6     | -31.3            | -46.5      |
| 3    | 143           | 19,519                 | 541,862 | 975.4       | 78.0    | 39.5         | 49%          | 12.7  | 26.5      | 28.8     | -29.4            | -75.9      |

Per outlet: gross profit about ₹3,600 a month; LTV ₹90,000; LTV ÷ CAC 8.5; payback 3 months. No break-even within 36 months, because the team grows with the business. The business is unit-profitable but company-unprofitable: it needs about ₹75 to 80 lakh of outside money over three years, and it reaches about ₹10 crore of order value and ₹78 lakh of revenue in year 3. Break-even arrives in year 4 at roughly 180 to 200 active outlets if the team stops growing.

### Aggressive: canteen-scale outlets, 10% commission, fast signing

New outlets a month: 6, then 15, then 30. Churn 3%. 1,000 orders per outlet a month at ₹200. Engineers: one from month 7, two from month 13, four from month 25.

| Year | Outlets (end) | Active customers (end) | Orders    | Order value | Revenue | Direct costs | Gross margin | Sales | Marketing | Salaries | Operating result | Cumulative |
| ---- | ------------- | ---------------------- | --------- | ----------- | ------- | ------------ | ------------ | ----- | --------- | -------- | ---------------- | ---------- |
| 1    | 61            | 15,082                 | 253,937   | 507.9       | 50.8    | 16.2         | 68%          | 11.3  | 13.1      | 10.2     | 0.0              | 0.0        |
| 2    | 196           | 51,867                 | 1,215,407 | 2,430.8     | 243.1   | 94.8         | 61%          | 19.1  | 57.9      | 28.8     | 42.5             | 42.5       |
| 3    | 442           | 120,641                | 3,141,658 | 6,283.3     | 628.3   | 244.1        | 61%          | 38.2  | 147.8     | 45.6     | 152.7            | 195.2      |

Per outlet: gross profit about ₹12,250 a month; LTV ₹4.1 lakh; payback under a month. Break-even in month 5; about ₹2 crore of cumulative profit by year 3 on ₹63 crore of order value. Read the assumptions before getting excited: 1,000 prepaid orders a month per outlet is a big canteen, not a café (HungerBox's average cafeteria does about 14,000 a month, but those are institution-contracted, every-vendor sites), and a 10% commission accepted by 442 outlets contradicts every price anchor in section 5. This scenario is what the institution model (9.4) looks like if it works, and it requires a sales team and capital the base case does not have.

### What the three stories have in common

- The per-order economics are fine in every scenario once an outlet is active; the problem is never the ₹7 to 15 per order. It is how many outlets activate, how many orders each does, and how long they stay.
- Salaries, not marketing, are the biggest cost line in every year of every scenario. A two-founder team that pays itself nothing can run the conservative or base case on under ₹40 lakh over three years; the moment it hires, the base case needs outside money.
- The number that decides which story comes true is orders per outlet per month: 250, 500 or 1,000. That number is measurable in a four-week pilot (Part E), and nothing else in this report should be believed until it has been measured.

## 14. The biggest reasons Juicy could fail, and what to do about each

Likelihood is this report's judgement from the evidence, not a measurement.

| #   | Risk                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Likelihood                    | The evidence                                                                                                                                                                                    | Mitigation                                                                                                                                                                               |
| --- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1   | **Customers don't care enough.** The queue is annoying but not painful enough to change a daily habit, or the kitchen is the real bottleneck so order-ahead saves nothing                                                                                     | High                          | No Indian measurement of canteen queues exists; real student complaints are about quality and price; takeaway inside the aggregators stayed at 0.5 to 2.5% of orders even when free             | Measure the queue before building anything else (section 18, experiment 1). Only pilot at outlets with a measured 8-minute-plus peak wait and a kitchen that can batch ahead             |
| 2   | **Restaurants don't care, or won't pay.** The outlet sees a 10% cut on customers who were already in the building                                                                                                                                             | High                          | Direct-channel prices are 0 to 3%; Zomato takeaway is free; canteen contractors sell at committee-fixed prices with 5 to 12% margins; MealPe went "commission-free" and still shows no traction | Drop the 10% commission as the default. Sell a flat fee or an institution fee; charge a commission only on provably new orders. Ask for money in week 4 of the pilot, not month 12       |
| 3   | **Existing competitors copy it.** Petpooja or DotPe add "prepaid pickup token" in a release; HungerBox or SpaceBasic move down-market; Zomato re-promotes takeaway                                                                                            | Medium                        | Swiggy copied Zomato Pickup in 50 days; Petpooja already sells tokens and QR for ₹20,000 a year; HungerBox "expanded into universities" in FY25                                                 | Make the moat the campus relationship and the counter workflow, not the app. Integrate into Petpooja rather than fight it. Sign a default-status agreement with each campus              |
| 4   | **Customer acquisition is too expensive.** Not on a campus: it is cheap                                                                                                                                                                                       | Low                           | Snackpass: 80% of Yale from flyers and a discount; ambassadors cost ₹3,000 to 15,000 a month; ₹40 of free food per first order pays back in two months                                          | Use QR at the counter, ambassadors and WhatsApp groups; never paid app-install ads                                                                                                       |
| 5   | **Restaurant acquisition is too expensive.** A field executive signs 5 to 12 outlets a month; one in three activate; 39% leave within a year                                                                                                                  | Medium-high                   | Thrive: one to two years to educate a market; Petpooja: 1 lakh outlets at ₹10,000 a year and still a loss                                                                                       | Sell to institutions (one signature, ten counters) or through a POS partner; measure CAC per retained outlet, not per signed one                                                         |
| 6   | **Low retention.** Students try it, then stop: exams, vacations, one late order                                                                                                                                                                               | High                          | 46% of apps uninstalled within 30 days; India shopping-app day-30 retention down 41%; Ritual lost 70% of traffic when routes broke; HungerBox fell 75% in the lockdown                          | Make the campus the daily trigger (every counter on the app, tokens called from it); build for the vacation calendar (sign offices too); never be late on a prepaid order                |
| 7   | **Low willingness to pay.** The real range may be ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month or ₹2 to 5 an order                                                                                                                                                                 | High                          | Petpooja at ₹10,000 a year per restaurant; SpaceBasic at ₹2.2 lakh per institution; HungerBox at ₹5.9 an order                                                                                  | Price low and spread the cost across institution, outlet and student (the hybrid). Keep the team tiny until the price is proven                                                          |
| 8   | **Poor unit economics.** ₹14.60 per order after the gateway at 10%; near zero at 2 to 3%                                                                                                                                                                      | Medium                        | Section 9 arithmetic; the Cashfree 0% promotion is capped at ₹20 lakh and ends March 2027                                                                                                       | Stay out of the money flow where possible (outlet's own UPI QR); use web push, not SMS; keep support self-serve                                                                          |
| 9   | **Network effects never develop.** Each campus starts from zero; no city-wide effect exists in this category                                                                                                                                                  | High                          | Ritual's 10,000 restaurants did not protect it; Snackpass' investors warned campus clusters do not transfer                                                                                     | Plan as a sales-led, campus-by-campus business, not a viral app; fund it accordingly                                                                                                     |
| 10  | **Restaurants don't want another platform.** They already run Zomato, Swiggy, a POS and maybe ONDC on one or two tablets                                                                                                                                      | High                          | Multi-homing is the norm (Swiggy 265,800 transacting restaurants; Petpooja 1 lakh)                                                                                                              | Appear inside the POS or on the staff's own phone; never a third tablet                                                                                                                  |
| 11  | **Regulatory and payment issues.** RBI aggregator rules forbid Juicy holding money; GST section 9(5) likely makes Juicy the GST payer on every order; DPDP by May 2027 with a 17-year-old-student trap; a customer "queue fee" next to UPI needs legal review | Medium                        | Section 8; Eternal carries ₹420 crore of GST demands                                                                                                                                            | Pilot out of the money flow; get a CA's written view on 9(5) for a pickup-only platform before any real payment; add an age gate                                                         |
| 12  | **Technology and integration difficulty.** Not the app (it exists and is well built); the integrations: payment aggregator with split settlement, POS, campus Wi-Fi allow-lists                                                                               | Low-medium                    | The codebase is solid; the roadmap's own risk table lists aggregator onboarding and KYC as "high, high"                                                                                         | Start the aggregator paperwork in month 1 of a go decision; integrate Petpooja before building a ledger                                                                                  |
| 13  | **Operational complexity.** Prepaid orders mean refunds, disputes, no-shows, "it's not ready", wrong pickups; support scales with orders                                                                                                                      | Medium-high                   | One ₹22,000 support executive per 1,500 orders a month at ₹14.60 contribution                                                                                                                   | Pay at the counter in the pilot; add prepayment only where no-shows are measured; token calling and a pickup corner at every outlet                                                      |
| 14  | **Large incumbents enter.** Zomato or Swiggy launch a campus pickup programme; HungerBox goes self-serve                                                                                                                                                      | Low-medium                    | Both giants built and sidelined pickup twice; their current bets are delivery and quick commerce; HungerBox sells managed services, not self-serve                                              | Be the one that already has the campus contracts when they look; sell to the institution, which is a sales motion the giants have not run                                                |
| 15  | **Building before validating.** The roadmap schedules ten phases of platform work before a paying café exists                                                                                                                                                 | High (it is the current plan) | The repository is six days old with a 33-item roadmap and no interview, queue count or price in it                                                                                              | Reorder the roadmap: pilot first, platform later (section 12 and Part E)                                                                                                                 |
| 16  | **Seasonality.** A campus empties for three to four months a year                                                                                                                                                                                             | Certain                       | Academic calendars; Ritual and HungerBox precedents                                                                                                                                             | Mix campuses with offices and hospitals; price annually, not monthly, for institutions                                                                                                   |
| 17  | **Being dearer than the counter.** Swiggy's Toing has taught students that app prices match or beat the counter; if Juicy must add 5% GST that the canteen does not charge, it is visibly dearer (found by the independent pass)                              | Medium-high                   | Toing's reported 33 million weekly users within a year (CLSA estimate); GST section 9(5)                                                                                                        | Never charge more than the counter price; get the accountant's view before launch; if a GST line is unavoidable, show it honestly in the pilot and measure whether students still switch |
| 18  | **The escrow product may not be available yet.** Razorpay's Route requires ₹40 lakh of prior turnover; Cashfree's 0% offer does not cover its split product (found by the independent pass and the repository's own payment research)                         | Medium-high                   | [Razorpay Route docs](https://razorpay.com/docs/payments/route/); `plans/PAYMENT_OPTIONS.md`                                                                                                    | Pilot with the outlet collecting its own money; ask both providers in writing (section 18, experiment 9) before writing any escrow code                                                  |

## 15. Can Juicy build a moat?

A moat is whatever stops a competitor from copying you and taking your customers. The honest answer today is that Juicy has none, and that the software can never be one: dozens of student projects on GitHub do what V0 does, and the aggregators proved they can ship pickup in weeks. What could become a moat, and what could not:

| Source of defensibility  | Today                                       | Could it become one?                                                                                                                              | Evidence                                                                         |
| ------------------------ | ------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Brand                    | None                                        | Weak at best. A campus brand ("the app our canteen uses") is possible; a city brand against Zomato and Swiggy is not                              | Eternal spent ₹1,972 crore on advertising in FY25                                |
| Network effects          | None                                        | Yes, but only inside one campus at a time (section 10). No city-wide effect exists in this category                                               | Snackpass; Ritual                                                                |
| Data                     | None                                        | Small: prep-time prediction and peak forecasting per outlet improve with history and reduce the outlet's waste. Useful, not defensible            | HungerBox sells insights but wins on contracts                                   |
| Technology               | A well-built, fast web app for cheap phones | A feature, not a moat; the first competitor with a good engineer matches it                                                                       | Low-end-device performance is real and rare, but copyable                        |
| Restaurant relationships | None yet                                    | Yes, if they become default-status agreements: "orders on this campus go through Juicy; tokens are called from the board"                         | Tapingo's dining contracts; HungerBox's 3-to-5-year contracts                    |
| Customer relationships   | None                                        | Weak without a wallet or stored value; students have nothing to lose by switching                                                                 | Yoyo's wallet; Grubhub+ Student; Snackpass points                                |
| Switching costs          | Near zero on both sides                     | Moderate if Juicy holds the outlet's menu history, its token workflow and its prep-time data, and the campus's wallet balances                    | Section 10                                                                       |
| Exclusive partnerships   | None                                        | Yes: an exclusive or default agreement with a college or canteen contractor, or a resale deal with a POS vendor                                   | SmartQ and Compass; Hangry and Transact                                          |
| Geographic density       | None                                        | The strongest available: own most counters on a campus, then the next. Hard to copy campus by campus, easy to copy in any single café             | Grubhub adds 60 campuses a year with a national sales force                      |
| Integrations             | None                                        | Yes: deep POS integration (Petpooja, UrbanPiper) and campus-ID or wallet integration are slow to build and slow to replace                        | Transact and CBORD own US campus ordering through the campus card                |
| Loyalty ecosystem        | None                                        | Possible later (a campus wallet, points across every counter); not a wedge                                                                        | Zomato Gold repriced three times                                                 |
| Money-handling rules     | Designed, not built                         | Modest: escrow until pickup, refunds when the outlet is at fault, paid on no-show, surplus resale. Regulated and fiddly, so most QR tools skip it | No Indian QR tool does this; Too Good To Go proves surplus resale works at scale |

Honest conclusion: there is no strong moat today and the product alone will never be one. The only defensible position in this category, everywhere it has been tried, is institutional: contracts that make the app the default on a campus, density across its counters, and integrations (POS, campus ID, wallet) that take months to replace. That position is built by a sales team, campus by campus, over years. If the founder is not prepared to run that kind of company, the moat question answers itself.

## 16. What happened to everyone who tried something like this

A startup benchmark is a company that attempted a similar thing. The pattern across more than 25 of them is unusually clear, so it is worth reading slowly.

### 16.1 Campus and order-ahead apps (the closest comparables)

| Company                    | What it did                                                                                                   | What happened                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Lesson for Juicy                                                                                                                                                                                           |
| -------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Tapingo (US)               | Order-ahead for pickup on college campuses from 2012, paid with meal-plan dollars                             | Grew to 150 or more campuses and 500,000 customers; sold to Grubhub for about $152 million in November 2018; the app was discontinued in 2019 and folded into Grubhub Campus Dining. [Grubhub 10-K FY2018](https://www.sec.gov/Archives/edgar/data/1594109/000156459019005487/grub-10k_20181231.htm)                                                                                                                                                                     | The exact idea built a real company. But what the buyer paid for was campus contracts, meal-plan integration and POS links, not the consumer app, which was shut                                           |
| Grubhub Campus Dining (US) | The successor: the university's dining operation is the customer; students get a free Grubhub+ membership     | 360 or more universities, 4.5 million students, 60 schools added for 2024-25. [Restaurant Dive, Aug 2024](https://www.restaurantdive.com/news/grubhub-expands-campus-dining-program-60-more-colleges/724855/)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | The student app is the free end of a business-to-business sale to the institution                                                                                                                          |
| Hangry (Canada)            | White-label campus ordering app, integrated with the campus card                                              | 180 or more campuses and $125 million of campus transactions a year; bought by the campus-card company Transact in July 2022; Transact itself was bought for $1.5 billion in 2024. [EdScoop](https://edscoop.com/hangry-transact-acquisition-campus-tech/)                                                                                                                                                                                                               | In mature markets campus ordering ends up as a feature of the campus-card and payments vendor                                                                                                              |
| Snackpass (US)             | Campus-first order-ahead with social gifting; started at Yale in 2017 with flyers and a "Happy Hour" discount | Reached 80% of Yale in one semester, 500,000 users across 13 college towns, 4.5 orders per user per month, about $60 million of order value, a 7% starting commission, and raised more than $95 million at a $400 million-plus valuation in 2021. Since 2022 it sells registers, kiosks and kitchen screens to restaurants. [Contrary Research](https://research.contrary.com/company/snackpass); [a16z](https://a16z.com/announcement/why-were-investing-in-snackpass/) | The only campus consumer app that scaled, and it still had to become restaurant software to keep growing. Its investors warned the "tight network clusters" of college towns "aren't replicated elsewhere" |

[Globe and Mail](https://www.theglobeandmail.com/business/article-ritual-cofounders-join-shopify/)                                                                                          | A 10,000-restaurant "network" did not protect it when the daily commute stopped. Order-ahead demand follows a daily route, not a network                                                                   |

[Crunchbase](https://www.crunchbase.com/organization/allset)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Standalone pickup apps have not survived in the US                                                                                                                                                         |

[PitchBook](https://pitchbook.com/profiles/company/58973-86)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      | Campus wallets got absorbed into payments companies                                                                                                                                                        |

[Access Group](https://www.theaccessgroup.com/en-gb/products/qikserve/)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Order-ahead became a feature, not a company                                                                                                                                                                |

| --------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| HungerBox (Bengaluru) | Founded 2015. In 2019 it described a business-to-business model earning about 10% commission from food vendors, with three-to-five-year client contracts as its defence. [Inc42, 2019](https://inc42.com/buzz/hungerbox-foodtech-first-mover-talks-about-profitability-expansion-and-ipo/) FY25: ₹885.9 crore of transactions, ₹82 crore revenue (a 9.3% take), ₹9.2 crore EBITDA, 14 crore orders, 831 live cafeterias, 270 clients, 39 cities; "expanded into universities and hospitals"; preparing to list. [YourStory, Nov 2025](https://yourstory.com/2025/11/hungerbox-posts-33-rise-in-gross-transaction-value-for-fy25) Arithmetic: average order about ₹63, revenue about ₹5.9 per order, about 14,000 orders a month per cafeteria. Its daily orders fell 75% in the 2020 lockdown. | The closest Indian comparable. It took a decade, $23 to 44 million of funding (sources differ) and 831 cafeterias to reach ₹9 crore of operating profit, selling to institutions on multi-year contracts. A 3,000-order-a-month campus café is one-fifth of an average HungerBox cafeteria |
