[MealPe](https://mealpe.app/)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | The direct Indian competitor is still tiny three years in                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Thrive (Mumbai)       | 3% commission direct ordering; 7,000 restaurants and 100 cities by late 2021; 35% bought by Jubilant FoodWorks. Its founder: "educating new markets requires 1-2 years". [afaqs](https://www.afaqs.com/news/mktg/can-online-restaurant-ordering-platform-thrive-now-make-a-dent-in-swiggy-and-zomatos-market-share)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            | Restaurants will adopt a 3% channel, slowly                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Petpooja (Ahmedabad)  | 1 lakh or more restaurants; FY25 revenue ₹101 crore (about ₹10,000 per restaurant per year) and a ₹21.4 crore loss. [Inc42, Apr 2026](https://inc42.com/features/petpooja-restaurants-the-missing-sales-trail-under-taxmens-lens/)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | Restaurant software in India reaches scale only at very low prices, and still loses money                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| Dineout               | Bought by Swiggy for $120 million in stock in 2022; Swiggy's dining unit now has 59,000 active restaurants and earns 0.9% of order value in operating profit                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                   | Restaurant-side businesses in India earn thin margins                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Box8 / EatClub        | Started by selling wraps to fellow students; survived with 250 or more cloud kitchens                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | A campus start works when the company owns the kitchen economics                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

### 16.5 What all of this means for Juicy

1. No standalone order-ahead consumer app survived as a standalone anywhere. The survivors became the restaurant's software (Snackpass, Toast, Owner.com) or the institution's vendor (Grubhub Campus, Transact, HungerBox, SmartQ).
2. The campus effect is real but local and temporary: Snackpass proves 75 to 80% of a campus can be won in months, and its own investors say that does not transfer off campus.
3. The Indian version of "network effect" is a multi-year institution contract. HungerBox's defence against Sodexo and Zomato was three-to-five-year contracts, not its app.
4. Pricing anchors everywhere are a flat monthly fee or a low per-order fee, often with a visible customer fee (Owner.com's 5%, Ritual's later $0.15 to $1.99 per order). Nobody made 10% of a ₹200 pickup order work as a standalone business.
5. The classic Indian failure is expanding before the unit economics work (TinyOwl, Dunzo). The classic survival is owning more of the counter (Toast) or the kitchen (Box8).

## 17. Interview plan: 30 non-leading questions

The rule for every question: never mention Juicy, never describe the feature, never ask "would you use X?". People say yes to be polite. Ask about what they did last week, what it cost them, and what they already tried. Facts about the past beat opinions about the future.

### 10 questions for restaurant and canteen owners or managers

1. Walk me through your busiest hour yesterday. What happened at the counter, minute by minute?
2. How many orders did you do on a normal weekday last week, and how many in the single busiest hour? How do you know that number?
3. When the queue gets long, what do customers do? What do you and your staff do?
4. How do you take orders and payments today, and roughly what share comes from each: counter, Zomato or Swiggy, WhatsApp, phone, a corporate or college arrangement?
5. What did you pay last month in platform commissions, software fees and payment fees? (Ask to see a statement or the app's payout screen.)
6. Which tool, app or platform did you last stop using, and what made you stop?
7. When a customer orders ahead and does not turn up, what happens to the food and the money today? How often does that happen in a week?
8. Who decides whether your outlet adopts new software: you, the owner, the college or facility manager, the contractor? What was the last such decision and how long did it take?
9. What is the most annoying part of running orders right now that you would pay to make go away? What monthly amount would be "obviously worth it", and what would be "too much"?
10. Have you tried online ordering for pickup before (your own app or website, Zomato takeaway, a WhatsApp menu, a QR code)? What happened to it?

### 10 questions for customers (students and office workers)

1. Tell me about the last time you bought lunch on campus or near the office. Where, what, how did you pay, and how long did it take from deciding to eating?
2. How many times last week did you skip a meal, eat somewhere else, or eat later because of a queue or a wait?
3. When you want food quickly, what do you actually do today? (walk, send a friend, order delivery, carry food from home)
4. Which apps did you use to order food in the last month? Which one the most, and why that one?
5. When you order through an app, what annoys you most? Tell me about the last time something went wrong.
6. Have you ever ordered ahead and picked up yourself (a café app, McDonald's, Domino's, Starbucks)? What made you do it, or not do it?
7. How do you feel about paying for food before you see it? What would make you nervous about that?
8. How much do you spend on food on a normal day on campus or at work? What is an "expensive" lunch for you?
9. Imagine a counter you buy from often let you order from your phone and collect when ready. What do you expect would go wrong?
10. What makes you delete or stop opening an app you downloaded? Give me the last example.

### 10 questions for counter and kitchen staff

1. What happens at the counter in the busiest fifteen minutes? Who does what?
2. How do you keep track of which order belongs to whom today: paper, memory, tokens, a screen?
3. What are the most common order mistakes, and when do they happen?
4. When you run out of an item during the rush, how do customers find out, and what do they do?
5. Do you use any screen, tablet or app for orders now? What is good and bad about it?
6. If orders started arriving from phones before the customer reached the counter, what would change in your work? What would worry you?
7. How does a customer prove an order is theirs when they collect it? Have there been wrong pickups or arguments?
8. How often does a customer who paid online come back with a complaint? What do you do then?
9. If a device showed the list of orders to prepare, where would it sit, and who would watch it?
10. What is the one change that would make your shift easier?

### How many interviews before deciding anything

| Who                                                 | How many                                                | Why this number                                                                                                    |
| --------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Owners / managers of campus and near-campus outlets | 15 to 20, across at least 3 campuses                    | Answers usually start repeating after 12 to 15 ("saturation"). Fewer than 10 and one loud person skews everything. |
| Customers                                           | 30 to 40 interviews, plus a short survey of 200 or more | Interviews find the reasons; the survey tells you how common each reason is.                                       |
| Staff                                               | 8 to 10                                                 | They know where the real friction is and they are the people who will have to use the order board.                 |

Decision rule before building more: at least 1 in 3 operators must name queues, peak-hour chaos or lost orders as a top-3 problem WITHOUT being prompted, and at least 1 in 3 customers must describe a wasted lunch break in the last two weeks. If neither bar is met, the problem is not painful enough and the product should change before any more code is written.

## 18. Cheap experiments to run before spending real money

Each experiment answers one question. Run them roughly in this order, except experiment 9, which belongs in week 1 before any real money moves; most cost almost nothing but your time. Thresholds are starting points, not laws: the point is to write the pass and fail numbers down BEFORE you run the test, so you cannot talk yourself into a "maybe".

| #   | Experiment                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             | Question it answers                                                                                           | Cost                                                                              | Time           | What to measure                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               | Success                                                                                                                                                  | Failure                                                                          | What you can conclude                                                                    |
| --- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1   | Queue study: stand at 3 to 5 campus or near-campus counters at lunch with a stopwatch and a tally sheet                                                                                                                                                                                                                                | Is there a physical queue problem at all, and how big                                                         | ₹0                                                                                | 3 days         | People in line every 5 minutes; wait time from joining to receiving food; share of the day's orders in the busiest 60 minutes; how many people look at the line and leave                                                                                                                                     | Average peak wait of 8 minutes or more at most outlets, and 30% or more of daily orders inside one hour                                                  | Average wait under 3 minutes, or lines that clear in a minute                    | Whether "skip the queue" is a real pain or a story                                       |
| 2   | Landing page and waitlist, promoted only in one campus's WhatsApp groups and Instagram                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                 | Will students lift a finger for the promise                                                                   | ₹0 to ₹2,000 (domain)                                                             | 1 week         | Visitors; phone-number signups; which outlet they name as "where I would use it"                                                                                                                                                                                                                              | 10% of visitors sign up and 150 or more names from one campus                                                                                            | Under 3% sign up                                                                 | Whether the pitch pulls at all, and which outlets students want it at                    |
| 3   | Poster with a QR code at a friendly counter: "Skip the queue: order from your phone" leading to a page that says "not live yet, tap if you want this" (fake door)                                                                                                                                                                      | Will people reach for it at the moment of pain                                                                | ₹500 of printing                                                                  | 1 week         | Scans per day ÷ customers per day; taps on "I want this"; repeat scans                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        | 5% or more of daily customers scan, 2% or more tap                                                                                                       | Under 1% scan                                                                    | Whether the pain is felt at the counter, not just in a survey                            |
| 4   | Outlet pre-signups: visit 15 outlets with a one-page offer and ask for a written yes to a one-month free pilot followed by a token fee (for example ₹500 a month)                                                                                                                                                                      | Will anyone commit anything, even small                                                                       | ₹0                                                                                | 2 weeks        | Yes with the fee; yes only if free; no; who the real decision-maker turned out to be                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | 4 or more of 15 say yes including the fee                                                                                                                | 1 or fewer say yes with the fee                                                  | Whether there is a buyer, and at what level of the organisation                          |
| 5   | Concierge MVP: one outlet, a WhatsApp number; students send orders by text, you type them into the staff board, they pay at the counter                                                                                                                                                                                                | Will people order ahead when it is slightly clunky but real                                                   | ₹0 plus your time                                                                 | 2 weeks        | Orders per day; share of the outlet's peak-hour orders; users who order 3 or more times; no-shows; what staff complain about                                                                                                                                                                                  | 10% of peak-hour orders by week 2 and 40% of users repeat                                                                                                | Under 3% of orders, or staff refuse to continue                                  | Whether the behaviour exists before any more software is built                           |
| 6   | Real-app pilot at that outlet, pay at the counter or via the outlet's own UPI QR                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                       | Does the software hold up, and does prepayment help or hurt                                                   | ₹0 to ₹10,000 (a cheap tablet for the counter)                                    | 3 to 4 weeks   | Everything in experiment 5 plus day-7 and day-30 retention, promised-time accuracy, order mistakes, the share who choose to prepay when offered; weekly orderers as a share of the campus's students in weeks 2 and 4, after a one-week launch offer and no discount afterwards (the independent pass's test) | 15% of peak orders by week 4; 30% of week-1 users still ordering in week 4; under 3% no-shows; 3% or more of students ordering weekly without a discount | Under 5% of orders, retention under 10%, or under 3% of students ordering weekly | Whether the product (not the idea) works, and whether prepayment is wanted               |
| 7   | Price reveal, inside experiments 4 and 6: run the pilot free; on day 15 show each outlet the real options (10%, 5% or 3% per order, or ₹2,000 a month flat) and record which it picks; ask the three price questions (a bargain at, expensive at, never at); at the end of the free month, actually ask for the money                  | What is it worth to them, and in which form                                                                   | ₹0                                                                                | runs alongside | The option each outlet picks; the three price points; how many pay after the free month; each owner's answer to "which of these orders would you not have got at the counter?"                                                                                                                                | Half or more of pilot outlets pay something after the free month, and two or more of five accept 5% or a flat fee                                        | Nobody pays, or fewer than two of five accept anything above 3%                  | Whether the business is a flat fee, a commission, or nothing                             |
| 8   | Single-campus launch: 3 to 5 outlets on one campus, posters, 3 to 5 student ambassadors, a free-item promotion for first orders                                                                                                                                                                                                        | Can this become a habit across a campus, not one counter                                                      | ₹30,000 to ₹60,000 (tablets, stipends, promotions)                                | 8 weeks        | Weekly active customers as a share of students who signed up; orders per active user; cross-outlet use; outlets paying; support messages per 100 orders                                                                                                                                                       | 2 or more outlets paying, 35% weekly active, 2 orders a week per active user                                                                             | Under 1 outlet paying or weekly active under 15%                                 | Whether to look for campus number two or change the plan                                 |
| 9   | Payments and GST check: apply to Razorpay Route and Cashfree Easy Split with real company documents and ask in writing whether a pre-revenue platform can hold settlements until pickup; ask a chartered accountant in writing whether a pickup-only platform owes 5% GST under section 9(5), both with and without handling the money | Can the planned payment design legally exist at launch, and would Juicy add a tax the counter does not charge | ₹0 for the applications; a few thousand rupees for the accountant (an assumption) | 1 week         | The written answers                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           | A split product is open to a pre-revenue platform, or the GST liability can be avoided while the outlet collects its own money                           | No split product, and 5% GST due on every order whoever collects it              | Whether the roadmap's payment design and pricing must change before any real-money pilot |

Two notes on experiment 6. A deep link to the outlet's own UPI ID (the same thing the sticker at the counter does) lets a customer pay the outlet directly from the web page, with the staff confirming the payment on their phone. It is manual, but it tests prepayment without any payment-aggregator contract. Check that the link works on PhonePe, Google Pay and Paytm first. And "pay at the counter" is not a cop-out: if people happily order ahead and pay on collection, the whole escrow and refund machinery in the roadmap can wait.

## 19. Three futures, and the early signals that tell them apart

### Scenario A: Juicy becomes a small, profitable niche business

What it looks like: 100 to 300 live counters across 15 to 40 Bengaluru campuses and the outlets just outside their gates, sold mostly to institutions at ₹1 to 3 lakh a year plus small per-order fees, a team of four to six, ₹1 to 3 crore of annual revenue, modest profit, no outside investors or one small angel round. Essentially a Bengaluru-only SpaceBasic for food, or a self-serve HungerBox for the long tail.

What causes it: the pilot shows a real but modest effect (15 to 30% of peak orders move to pre-order), outlets pay a flat fee but refuse a commission, each campus takes two to three months to win, and the founders decide a good small company is better than a failed big one.

Early signals (months 1 to 6): outlets pay after the free month; week-4 retention around 30%; no-shows under 3%; owners refer neighbours; but orders per outlet plateau at 300 to 600 a month and every new campus starts from zero.

### Scenario B: Juicy becomes a large Indian restaurant-tech or campus-food platform

What it looks like: the default ordering and payment rail across hundreds of campuses, hospitals and office parks in several cities, with a campus wallet, POS integrations and institution contracts; about 1,000 institutional sites and ₹50 to 100 crore of revenue, roughly HungerBox's size today (831 cafeterias and ₹82 crore of revenue in FY25); an exit to a facility operator (Compass, Sodexo), a POS company (Petpooja), or an aggregator, as every comparable did. Not a consumer food app competing with Swiggy.

What causes it: the pilot shows canteen-scale volumes (800 to 1,500 orders a month per counter) and 60%-plus campus penetration within a semester, like Snackpass at Yale; institutions sign multi-year contracts; the hybrid pricing clears ₹5,000-plus per counter per month; the founders raise ₹5 to 15 crore to build a sales team and survive 24 months of campus-by-campus expansion; a POS vendor or facility operator partners rather than competes.

Early signals (months 1 to 12): 50%-plus of a campus's students order in the first semester; three or more campuses signed by institution contract, not by individual cafés; orders per counter above 800 a month; a second city's first campus goes live without the founders present; a POS vendor asks to integrate.

### Scenario C: Juicy fails to find product-market fit

What it looks like: a polished app, two or three outlets using it lightly, students ordering once and not returning, owners declining to pay, the founders spending months on the payment-aggregator paperwork and the multi-store platform for customers who never arrive, and the project ending within 12 to 18 months, like MealPe's silent counters or Allset's closure.

What causes it: the queue turns out to be 3 minutes, not 10; the kitchen is the bottleneck; prepaid orders arrive late and the first bad experience ends the habit; outlets see a tool that moves their own customers to a phone and charges them for it; the roadmap is followed in its current order (platform first, customers later).

Early signals (months 1 to 3): a measured peak wait under 4 minutes; fewer than 1 in 3 owners mention the rush unprompted; under 3% of an outlet's peak orders through the app after two weeks of real use; nobody pays after the free month; the founders are still writing ledger code while no outlet is live.

### How to read the signals

The same pilot separates all three. Measure the queue, run one outlet for four weeks, ask for money. If the share of peak orders is under 5%, it is C. If it is 15 to 30% with a flat fee paid, it is A. If it is above 50% with canteen volumes and an institution asking for the whole campus, it might be B, and only then is it worth raising money and building the platform in the roadmap.

## 20. Final assessment: the strongest evidence on each side

No score, no rank. For each dimension, the single strongest piece of evidence for the opportunity and the single strongest against it.

| Dimension              | Strongest evidence for                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          | Strongest evidence against                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| ---------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| Problem severity       | Captive-site pre-ordering is a real, paid-for category: HungerBox does 13 million orders a month, about 7.5 per user, because peak-hour queues in short breaks are a genuine problem                                                                            | Nobody in India has measured a canteen queue; every source asserting it is a vendor or a student with an app; real student complaints are about food quality and price                                                                                                                                                                          |
| Customer demand        | Where the canteen makes order-ahead the default, people use it several times a week (HungerBox, SmartQ); the phones, data and UPI are ready; delivery fees are the one thing users resent                                                                       | Pickup offered as an option inside Zomato and Swiggy reached 0.5 to 2.5% of orders even at zero commission; half of new apps are gone in a month; the money is moving towards delivery; Swiggy's Toing has taught students to expect app prices at or below the counter                                                                         |
| Restaurant demand      | A documented, organised revolt against 24 to 35% aggregator costs in Bengaluru in July-August 2026; owners will listen to a cheaper channel                                                                                                                     | Every grievance in that revolt is about delivery; pickup is already free (Zomato takeaway) or 1 to 3% (DotPe, Thrive); canteen contractors sell at committee-fixed prices with 5 to 12% margins                                                                                                                                                 |
| Market size            | 1,148 colleges and an estimated 0.7 to 1.1 million students in Bengaluru alone; 46,000 colleges and 4.5 crore students nationally; both passes independently put the campus-only pool at about ₹160 to 170 crore a year of platform revenue at a 10% commission | At realistic volumes, revenue per outlet is ₹20,000 to 1.1 lakh a year; the Bengaluru SOM is ₹0.2 to 3.3 crore; the roadmap's own pilot is ₹7 to 24 lakh a year; at the 2 to 3% outlets actually pay for pickup, the India pool is ₹35 to 50 crore                                                                                              |
| Competition            | The giants do not want the counter; cafeteria operators will not sell to a single canteen; the clones have no traction, so the long tail is open                                                                                                                | HungerBox, SmartQ, GoKhana and SpaceBasic are all headquartered in Bengaluru and already hold the campuses with budgets; Rapido Ownly (about 25,000 Bengaluru restaurants) owns the "cheaper channel" slot in the city; Swiggy's Toing targets the same students and Deskeats the same offices; Petpooja sells tokens and QR for ₹20,000 a year |
| Monetisation potential | A hybrid (a campus fee from the college plus a small per-order fee from the outlet) clears costs if Juicy stays out of the money flow; charging students nothing (founder decision 2) removes the main reason for them not to switch                            | The planned 10% commission has the clearest evidence against it of anything in the plan; payments earn nothing; HungerBox earns about ₹5.9 an order, and Petpooja about ₹10,000 a year per restaurant while still losing money                                                                                                                  |
| Scalability            | Campus density is a repeatable playbook (Tapingo 5 to 150 campuses; Grubhub 60 a year)                                                                                                                                                                          | It is a slow, sales-led, business-to-business motion; each campus starts from zero; campuses empty for a third of the year                                                                                                                                                                                                                      |
| Unit economics         | About ₹7 to 15 of gross profit per order; a signed outlet pays back in about 3 months; a campus customer in about 2                                                                                                                                             | One in three outlets activate and 39% churn in a year; the 0% gateway promotion is a one-off; the base case needs ₹75 to 80 lakh over three years and does not break even inside them                                                                                                                                                           |
| Network effects        | Real inside one campus (the tenth counter makes the habit)                                                                                                                                                                                                      | None across a city; multi-homing is universal; Ritual's 10,000-restaurant network collapsed in a month when routes broke                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| Moat                   | Possible through institution contracts, campus density, POS and campus-ID integrations, and money-handling rules nobody else builds                                                                                                                             | None today; the app is a weekend project for a good student; the giants ship pickup in weeks                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| Execution difficulty   | The hard engineering (fast on cheap phones, stock-safe orders, security) is done well already                                                                                                                                                                   | The hard parts are not engineering: field sales, institution procurement, aggregator KYC per café, GST remittance, refunds and disputes, vacations                                                                                                                                                                                              |
| Capital requirements   | A two-founder pilot on one campus costs under ₹2 lakh; scenario A can be bootstrapped                                                                                                                                                                           | Scenario B needs ₹5 to 15 crore and 24 months; restaurant software raised $0.5 million in India in 2026; Curefoods could not IPO                                                                                                                                                                                                                |
| Long-term opportunity  | Being the ordering and payment rail of Indian campuses and institutions, a category that produced one profitable Bengaluru company and one Compass acquisition                                                                                                  | Every comparable ended as a feature inside a POS, a payments company or a facility operator, not as an independent consumer brand                                                                                                                                                                                                               |

### What would need to be true for Juicy to become a large company

1. A measured peak queue of 8 minutes or more at most target counters, and kitchens that can batch ahead.
2. 50% or more of a campus ordering through the app within a semester, with orders per counter above 800 a month.
3. Institutions, not individual cafés, signing multi-year contracts that make Juicy the default on campus.
4. A hybrid price that clears ₹5,000 or more per counter per month without a 10% commission.
5. Either a POS vendor or a facility operator choosing to partner rather than compete.
6. ₹5 to 15 crore of patient capital and a founder willing to run a field-sales company for three years.

### What evidence to collect before investing serious time or money

Queue waits with a stopwatch at five counters; fifteen owner interviews with the questions in section 17; one outlet's four-week pilot showing the share of peak orders, week-4 retention and no-shows; the first rupee actually paid by an outlet; a chartered accountant's written view on GST section 9(5) for a pickup-only platform; a written answer from Razorpay or Cashfree on whether a pre-revenue platform can use their split-settlement product; and a check of whether Zomato and Swiggy still offer pickup on the target campus. Everything else in the roadmap can wait for those seven facts.

## Part C: Independent second opinion: "Why might Juicy fail?" (Pass 2)

This pass was run separately from Part B. It was given only the neutral product fact sheet (what the code does and what the roadmap plans), never Pass 1's findings, and one question: why might this startup fail? It was asked to try to disprove the idea, but also to say honestly what surprised it in Juicy's favour. Its view is summarised here in plain words, with its sources. Where a fact in it was later checked and corrected, the correction is in Part D.

### C.1 Its verdict, in three sentences

The customer problem (queues at campus counters) is real but small, and the chosen way to make money from it (10% of a ₹200 order, paid by the restaurant) is the weakest possible one in a market where Swiggy, Rapido and now Flipkart are racing commissions towards zero. The product is not meaningfully different from Zomato Pickup (2018), Zomato's zero-commission takeaway (2020), the campus incumbents (HungerBox, SmartQ, MealPe) or Swiggy's student app Toing (2025-26), except that it is smaller. It can survive as a small software business for a few dozen canteens; as a venture-scale platform it most likely fails, and the top reasons are commercial, not technical.

### C.2 Its answers to the six questions

**1. Will restaurants pay?** Its view: campus canteens and small cafés will not pay 10% for pickup orders they already get at the counter. They might pay a flat ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month, or 2 to 3% on genuinely new orders, but only if the channel visibly brings orders they would not otherwise have. Every pickup-only benchmark is cheaper than 10%: Zomato takeaway 0% with the gateway fee absorbed ([Zomato blog, 2020](https://www.zomato.com/blog/takeaway/)), Swiggy's 2021 direct-ordering pilot 2.5% ([Inc42, Jul 2021](https://inc42.com/buzz/swiggy-pilots-direct-ordering-for-restaurants-amidst-backlash-from-industry/)), Thrive 3%, DotPe low single digits, Rapido Ownly 0%. Two facts it found decisive: the Bengaluru associations' own proposal during the 2026 standoff was a 22% cap on delivery commissions for new partners, with 40 to 45% total deductions called the real problem ([Moneycontrol, 7 Aug 2026](https://www.moneycontrol.com/news/business/bengaluru-restaurants-defer-swiggy-zomato-boycott-till-august-31-after-marathon-talks-propose-commission-cap-13998270.html)); and restaurants still did not leave the aggregators even at those deductions, because "that's where the customers are" (a Bengaluru owner kept less than 40% of ₹4 lakh of Zomato sales and would not delist; [Mint, Sep 2025](https://www.livemint.com/industry/restaurants-food-delivery-apps-margins-swiggy-zomato-bulk-orders-aggregator-platforms-11758455461794.html)). Restaurants pay for demand, not for low fees. Margins are thin and got thinner: NRAI's president said in May 2026 that a 60% LPG price rise pushed restaurants into "negative gross margins" for weeks ([Fortune India, May 2026](https://www.fortuneindia.com/business-news/restaurants-running-out-of-room-to-absorb-costs-says-nrai-president-wow-sees-uneven-impact/135613)). And the institutional model is thin even at scale: HungerBox's ₹885.9 crore of transactions produced ₹9.2 crore of operating profit in FY25, about 1% ([ET HospitalityWorld, Nov 2025](https://hospitality.economictimes.indiatimes.com/news/operations/food-and-beverages/hungerbox-delivers-strong-fy25-with-rs-886-crore-gtv-47-ebitda-growth/125583072)).

**2. Will customers switch?** Its view: students will try it, use it when the queue is long, and drop it the week the queue is short. Order-ahead at scale in India exists only inside big-brand apps bundled with discounts and loyalty. The aggregators' pickup stayed marginal and never earned a line in their shareholder letters; Swiggy's 2025-26 letters list Bolt, 99-Store, Deskeats, Eat Right and Toing, with no pickup product. What does move students is price: Swiggy's Toing ("zero platform fees, no packaging charges, prices that match or beat dine-in menus", aimed at "Gen Z, college students and first-time earners" and launched in "key education hubs"; [Fortune India, Feb 2026](https://www.fortuneindia.com/business-news/swiggys-toing-enters-delhi-ncr-bets-on-zero-fee-model-to-disrupt-food-delivery-market/130700)) reportedly reached about 33 million weekly active users, 96% of Zomato's, within a year, with 14.5 million downloads in one quarter (CLSA estimate reported by [Moneycontrol, 9 Sep 2026](https://www.moneycontrol.com/news/business/startup/swiggy-s-toing-user-base-now-almost-as-large-as-zomato-s-clsa-14025987.html)). The student segment has just been trained, at scale, to expect app prices at or below the counter. Juicy may be dearer than walking up, because an order through an e-commerce platform can carry 5% GST that the canteen does not charge at its counter (point 5). Abroad, campus order-ahead caught on where the campus meal plan ran through the app (Tapingo; Grubhub Campus), a structural reason to switch that Indian colleges do not provide; and office order-ahead collapsed when the habit broke (Ritual).

Its own switching estimate, stated as an assumption to test: on a campus of 3,000 students with one busy counter, 10 to 20% will register in a launch month with a promotion, and 3 to 6% will still order weekly three months later without a discount. That is 90 to 180 weekly orderers per campus, and it is the number to measure, not sign-ups.

**3. Who already does this?** Its competitor table adds consumer-side players to the institutional ones:

- **Swiggy Toing** (above), in Bengaluru since March 2026; restaurants complain of being listed without consent and losing price control ([Mint, Jun 2026](https://www.livemint.com/companies/news/restaurants-food-delivery-app-swiggy-toing-11781151815642.html)). Swiggy says it can add a need-state "at a fraction of the cost a new player would incur" ([Swiggy Q1 FY27 letter](https://www.swiggy.com/corporate/wp-content/uploads/2026/07/Q1-FY2027-Shareholder-letter.pdf)).
- **Swiggy Deskeats**, an office-meal proposition in 7,000 or more tech parks across 30 cities ([Swiggy Q2 FY26 letter](https://www.swiggy.com/corporate/wp-content/uploads/2025/10/Q2-FY2026-Shareholder-letter.pdf)): the office half of Juicy's audience is already served.
- **Rapido Ownly**, zero commission with offline menu prices, now about 25,000 Bengaluru restaurant partners and about 50,000 orders a day, expanding to all metros from October to December 2026 ([Mint, 26 Sep 2026](https://www.livemint.com/companies/news/why-your-100-meal-costs-250-online-and-how-rapido-wants-to-change-that-11790418590126.html)).
- **Flipkart "Eat In"**, being tested with employees in Bengaluru ([Entrackr, Sep 2026](https://entrackr.com/news/flipkart-starts-employee-testing-of-eat-in-food-delivery-service-in-bengaluru-12510648)), reportedly targeting a 10% commission: a fourth deep-pocketed player in Juicy's home city.
- **ONDC food**, in "steady decline through 2025" after incentives were cut ([FE, Jul 2026](https://www.financialexpress.com/business/news/flipkart-picks-standalone-route-for-food-delivery/4286636/)); Inc42 expects it to "fade away" ([Inc42, Dec 2025](https://inc42.com/features/why-2026-wont-be-an-easy-meal-for-food-delivery-platforms/)). Lesson: subsidised low-commission networks do not hold restaurants once the subsidy stops.
- The institutional players are the same as in Part B (HungerBox, SmartQ, MealPe), plus student hobby clones. Abroad: Tapingo to Grubhub Campus, which DoorDash agreed on 15 September 2026 to buy for $300 million, live at more than 450 US colleges ([DoorDash investor relations](https://ir.doordash.com/news/news-details/2026/DoorDash-and-Wonder-Announce-Strategic-Partnership/default.aspx)); Snackpass, whose homepage now sells "the creator marketing platform for local brands" plus POS; Ritual's collapse; Hangry sold to the campus-payments company Transact.

Its reading: the field is crowded above Juicy (Toing, Ownly, Flipkart, Zomato pickup at zero) and beside it (HungerBox, SmartQ, MealPe). The only empty square is order-ahead for the small, uncontracted canteen that no institutional caterer wants, and it is empty because it is small.

**4. How big is the market?** Its own bottom-up estimate, campuses only. Facts from the AISHE 2022-23 report: 46,624 colleges, 1,213 universities, 4.46 crore students; only 18.2% of colleges have more than 1,000 students (8,128 colleges); Bengaluru Urban has 1,107 colleges, the most of any district ([AISHE 2022-23](https://cdnbbsr.s3waas.gov.in/s392049debbe566ca5782a3045cf300a3c/uploads/2026/07/20260708401535366.pdf)). Assumptions: order-ahead only makes sense where there is a queue, so about 9,300 campuses with more than 1,000 students, holding 60% of students (2.68 crore); 40% buy something on a given day, 200 campus days a year, ₹80 average daily spend (most canteen tickets are ₹30 to 120); 10% of that spend could move to prepaid order-ahead.

| Step                                                        | India                                                | Bengaluru (9 lakh students)           |
| ----------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------- |
| Campus food spend a year                                    | 2.68 crore × 40% × 200 × ₹80 ≈ ₹17,150 crore         | 9 lakh × 40% × 200 × ₹80 ≈ ₹576 crore |
| Order-ahead-able (10%)                                      | ≈ ₹1,715 crore of orders                             | ≈ ₹58 crore of orders                 |
| Revenue pool at a 10% commission                            | ≈ ₹170 crore a year, shared by every campus platform | ≈ ₹5.8 crore a year                   |
| Revenue pool at the 2 to 3% outlets actually pay for pickup | ₹35 to 50 crore a year                               | ₹1.2 to 1.7 crore a year              |

If Juicy won a heroic 20% of Bengaluru campus order-ahead it would earn about ₹1.2 crore a year at 10%. It treats offices as mostly closed (Deskeats in 7,000 tech parks; cafeterias contracted to HungerBox, SmartQ and Sodexo). Conclusion: a fine bootstrapped business and a poor venture one, and it exists only if questions 1 and 2 go Juicy's way.

**5. Does ₹20 an order work?** Its per-order model (its assumptions marked):

| Line                                                                          | 10,000 orders a month | 3,000 orders a month | Basis                                                |
| ----------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------- | -------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------- |
| Commission revenue (10% × ₹200)                                               | ₹20.00                | ₹20.00               | Fact sheet                                           |
| Payment gateway 2% plus 18% GST                                               | −₹4.72                | −₹4.72               | [Razorpay pricing](https://razorpay.com/pricing/)    |
| Split settlement 0.1 to 0.25%                                                 | −₹0.30                | −₹0.30               | Razorpay, Cashfree                                   |
| Refunds and disputes                                                          | −₹1.10                | −₹1.10               | Assumption: 2% of orders refunded plus 0.5% goodwill |
| Notifications                                                                 | −₹0.30                | −₹0.30               | Assumption                                           |
| Hosting ₹20,000 a month                                                       | −₹2.00                | −₹6.67               | Assumption                                           |
| Support, one person at ₹30,000 a month                                        | −₹3.00                | −₹10.00              | Assumption                                           |
| Sales and onboarding, ₹8,000 per outlet for 10 outlets, spread over 12 months | −₹0.67                | −₹2.22               | Assumption                                           |
| Contribution per order                                                        | +₹7.91                | −₹5.01               |                                                      |
| Team of two to three at ₹2 lakh a month                                       | −₹2 lakh              | −₹2 lakh             | Assumption                                           |
| Monthly result                                                                | loss of ₹1.2 lakh     | loss of ₹2.15 lakh   |                                                      |

Break-even: about ₹2.57 lakh of fixed costs a month divided by (₹20 − ₹6.42 of variable cost) is about 18,900 orders a month, or 630 a day, at a 10% commission. At 15% it is about 10,900. At 3% (₹6 of revenue, less than the ₹6.42 of variable cost) there is no break-even at ₹200 tickets while Juicy handles the money. Three structural problems, in its words:

1. **Small tickets kill percentage models.** Zomato and Swiggy solved this with a fixed customer fee (now ₹14.90 and ₹17.58 an order). Juicy's plan rules out a customer fee.
2. **The 5% GST wedge.** Since January 2022 an e-commerce operator must charge and pay the 5% GST on "restaurant service", defined to include "mess, canteen" ([India Briefing, Jan 2022](https://www.india-briefing.com/news/key-gst-changes-india-applicable-from-january-1-2022-24039.html/)). Many canteens are below the GST threshold or price GST-inclusive at the counter. If the customer bears the 5%, the ₹200 order costs ₹210 on Juicy versus ₹200 at the counter, a visible reason not to switch. If the canteen bears it, Juicy's real cost to the canteen is 15%, not 10%. Whether the operator is liable even when the canteen is unregistered (CBIC Circular 167/23/2021) must be confirmed with a chartered accountant before launch; the pass could not fetch the circular and its sources read it differently.
3. **Escrow is not free or simple.** Under the RBI's September 2025 directions a non-bank cannot hold customer money without authorisation (₹15 crore net worth rising to ₹25 crore). The practical route is a licensed aggregator's split product, but Razorpay's Route now requires the platform to show more than ₹40 lakh of domestic turnover in FY25 or FY26 through GST returns, and disabled non-compliant users after 31 December 2025 ([Razorpay Route docs](https://razorpay.com/docs/payments/route/)). A pre-revenue startup may simply not qualify for the escrow mechanism its roadmap depends on.

**6. Is Juicy different?** Its view: another food app, with better engineering than most and a narrower use case than all. The engineering choices are genuinely good (fast on cheap phones, stock races settled in the database, prices from the server, pickup tokens and a secret code). None is a reason a customer or canteen chooses Juicy over the alternatives. It adds a sharp point about the roadmap's "surplus deals": reselling uncollected prepaid food only pays if no-shows are frequent, so it is a feature that only earns money if the core experience is bad. The one defensible wedge it sees: a counter-side operations tool that the canteen pays for as software (tokens, kitchen board, stock, pause, end-of-day reconciliation), with ordering as the hook, following the Owner.com and Olo pattern. That is a different company from the commission marketplace in the plan.

### C.3 Its top 10 reasons Juicy could fail, ranked

Likelihood: high means more likely than not within 18 months; medium 25 to 50%; low under 25%.

| Rank | Reason                                                                                                            | Likelihood                                                                                                                                              | What would have to be true for it not to happen                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                        |
| ---- | ----------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |
| 1    | Restaurants won't pay 10% for orders they already get, and already have a free pickup channel                     | High                                                                                                                                                    | Juicy brings provably new orders and prices below 3 to 5%, or charges a flat monthly fee the canteen values as labour saved                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                            |
| 2    | Customers don't care enough, and retention decays once novelty and promotions stop                                | High                                                                                                                                                    | A campus where the queue is reliably 15 minutes or more at every break, with 5% or more of students ordering weekly without any discount after month three                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                             |
| 3    | Unit economics don't close at ₹200 tickets and 10%                                                                | High                                                                                                                                                    | A customer fee of ₹5 to 10 an order, tickets above ₹400, or a software fee replacing the commission                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                    |
| 4    | Incumbents copy it or crush it on price                                                                           | High that the segment is contested; medium that an incumbent explicitly adds campus pickup, because it is too small for them, which is also the problem | Campus canteens contractually closed to aggregators (in which case institutional caterers are the competitors)                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                         |
| 5    | Restaurant acquisition is slow and expensive for a two-to-three-person team                                       | Medium-high                                                                                                                                             | Self-serve onboarding a canteen owner completes in an hour, and referrals between canteens on the same campus                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| 6    | Customer acquisition is expensive next to ₹20 of revenue                                                          | Medium                                                                                                                                                  | Acquisition through the canteen itself (a QR at the counter during the queue, at almost no cost) and zero paid marketing                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                               |
| 7    | The best campuses are already taken by institutional caterers, and single-canteen campuses give no network effect | Medium-high                                                                                                                                             | Target the long tail of mid-size colleges without a caterer, and multi-outlet food courts rather than single canteens                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                  |
| 8    | Regulation and payments: GST as an e-commerce operator, RBI aggregator rules, FSSAI, DPDP                         | Low for a fatal event; medium-high for damage to the model                                                                                              | A licensed aggregator's split product from day one, no self-held money; GST and FSSAI status checked per canteen; minimal data. (FSSAI issued Swiggy a prohibition order over Toing's licence in July 2026, resolved in three days: a separate platform needs its own correct licence; [Mint, Jul 2026](https://www.livemint.com/companies/news/swiggy-fssai-prohibition-order-toing-app-licence-11783688463770.html)) |
| 9    | Operations: no-shows, late orders, refunds, disputes, cold food                                                   | Medium                                                                                                                                                  | Pickup windows short enough that no-shows stay under 2%, and the canteen carrying no-show risk, which it accepts only if Juicy's fee is small                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                          |
| 10   | The counter does not want another screen                                                                          | Medium; the product's engineering discipline is the best mitigation                                                                                     | The staff board runs on the canteen's existing phone, prints tokens on a ₹3,000 Bluetooth printer, and syncs stock without a POS integration                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                                           |

### C.4 What surprised it in Juicy's favour

- Campus order-ahead is a real, valued category abroad right now: DoorDash is paying $300 million for Grubhub Campus Dining. The exit path exists, as infrastructure sold to institutions and payment companies, not as a consumer marketplace.
- Bengaluru is the best district in India for this idea on paper: more colleges than any other, in the state with the highest college density.
- No delivery cost is a genuine structural advantage: Swiggy's delivery cost is 12 to 13% of the order value; pickup has none.
- UPI's new 0.4% fee does not touch ₹200 orders, and the gateway promotions make a pilot cheap.
- Restaurants are angrier at aggregators than at any time since 2021, and the consent scandals create an opening for a transparent, opt-in, low-fee channel, if Juicy prices like Thrive, not like Zomato.
- Price-first students do move en masse: Toing proves the segment is reachable. The open question is whether anything other than price moves them.

### C.5 Its overall verdict and its three cheapest tests

As described in the fact sheet, a commission marketplace for pickup at ₹200 tickets, Juicy most likely fails commercially, not technically. The realistic good outcome is a small, profitable software-plus-payments tool for uncontracted canteens charging a flat ₹1,500 to 3,000 a month plus 1 to 2% for payments, sold campus by campus in Bengaluru, reaching a few crore a year and possibly bought by a caterer, a POS company or a payments company. The venture-scale outcome ("Grubhub Campus for India") requires owning the campus payment layer (meal plans, hostel mess, ID cards), which most Indian colleges do not have and which HungerBox and SmartQ already sell to the ones that do.

Its three cheapest tests:

1. **The price-reveal test** (restaurants; two weeks; about ₹5,000). Offer five Bengaluru canteens the live product at zero commission for a month, then on day 15 tell them the real price will be 10%. Count how many stay at 10%, at 5%, at 3%, or prefer ₹2,000 a month flat. If fewer than two of five accept 10%, reason 1 is confirmed. Ask each owner: "Which of these orders would you not have got at the counter?"
2. **The no-discount retention test** (customers; six weeks; about ₹10,000). On one campus, launch with a QR at the counter and one week of ₹20 off, then nothing. Measure weekly orderers as a share of enrolment in weeks 2, 4 and 6, and the median queue length at launch with a stopwatch. If week-6 weekly orderers are under 3% of students, or the queue is under 8 minutes, reason 2 is confirmed. Show the real price, including any 5% GST line, so the gap with the counter is visible.
3. **The payments and GST test** (regulation; one week; about ₹0). Before writing any escrow code, apply to Razorpay Route and Cashfree Easy Split with real company documents and ask in writing whether a pre-revenue platform can hold restaurant settlements until pickup; and get a chartered accountant's written answer on whether Juicy owes 5% GST on orders from an unregistered canteen. If the answers are "no" and "yes", the payment design and pricing must change before any pilot.

If all three come back positive (canteens accept 5% or more, students stay above 5% weekly without discounts, and the payments path is open), it would revise its verdict from "likely fails" to "a credible small business with a real exit path".

## Part D: Where the two passes agree and disagree, and what to believe

Pass 1 (Part B) and Pass 2 (Part C) were run separately; Pass 2 never saw Pass 1's findings. So when they agree, that is the strongest evidence in this report: two routes to the same answer. When they disagree, it shows either where the evidence is thin or where one pass found something the other missed. This part compares them on the six topics you asked about, then lists the facts they reported differently and how each was settled.

### D.1 At a glance

| Topic                         | Pass 1 (full study)                                                                                                                                                                                    | Pass 2 (independent)                                                                                                                                                                                                  | Verdict                                                                                    |
| ----------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------ |
| Restaurant willingness to pay | Flat ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month, or ₹2 to 5 an order, or an institution fee of ₹50,000 to 3 lakh a year; 10% only on provably new orders; canteens on college-fixed prices cannot pay a commission at all | 0 to 3% for digitised walk-up demand; 8 to 10% only on provably new orders; or flat ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month; restaurants pay for demand, not for low fees                                                             | **Agree.** Two independent routes to the same range                                        |
| Customer switching            | People use order-ahead when the outlet makes it the default; pickup as an option failed (0.5 to 2.5% of aggregator orders); success bar 15% of peak orders by week 4                                   | Students try it, use it when the queue is long, drop it when it is short; 3 to 6% of students still ordering weekly after three months without discounts; Toing has trained students on price                         | **Agree on direction; Pass 2 more pessimistic.** Pass 2's price point is new and important |
| Competitors                   | Mostly business-to-business: HungerBox, SmartQ, GoKhana, SpaceBasic hold the funded campuses; Petpooja and DotPe own the counter software; the clones have no traction                                 | Adds the consumer price war: Toing, Deskeats, Ownly, Flipkart; ONDC fading                                                                                                                                            | **Complementary.** Both are right; Juicy is squeezed from above and from beside            |
| Market size                   | India ₹100 to 1,200 crore a year counting campuses, cafés, quick-service counters and offices; campus-only base ₹162 crore; Bengaluru TAM ₹11 to 200 crore, obtainable ₹0.2 to 3.3 crore               | Campuses only: India about ₹170 crore at 10% (₹35 to 50 crore at 2 to 3%); Bengaluru ₹1 to 6 crore; offices treated as closed                                                                                         | **Same arithmetic, different scope.** Plan on the narrower campus-only number              |
| Unit economics                | About ₹14.60 per order after payment costs at 10%; 10,000 orders a month covers about three junior salaries; the base case needs ₹75 to 80 lakh over three years                                       | About ₹7.91 per order at 10,000 orders after spreading fixed costs; break-even about 18,900 orders a month for a team of two or three; none at 3% while handling the money; a 5% GST wedge; escrow may be unavailable | **Agree.** Pass 2 adds refunds, the GST wedge and the escrow gate                          |
| Differentiation               | Inside a captive canteen Juicy is the outlet's ordering rail, not "another food app"; as a many-restaurant marketplace it is a subset of what the aggregators shipped                                  | "Another food app, with better engineering than most and a narrower use case than all"; the defensible wedge is a counter-side operations tool sold as software                                                       | **Same conclusion in different words**                                                     |

### D.2 Topic by topic

**Restaurant willingness to pay.** Both passes reached the same range without seeing each other: a flat ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month, or a few rupees or a few percent per order, with 8 to 10% only defensible on orders the outlet would not otherwise have got. Both name the 10% commission as the weakest assumption in the plan. Pass 2 adds two things worth keeping. First, restaurants stay on Zomato and Swiggy even at 40 to 45% total deductions "because that's where the customers are", which means a low fee alone does not attract them; demand does. Second, the GST wedge: if Juicy must add 5% GST that an unregistered canteen does not charge, a 10% commission effectively becomes 15% for the canteen. **What settles it:** the price-reveal test (section 18, experiment 7).

**Customer switching.** Both say the same thing about direction: order-ahead works when the outlet makes it the default and fails when it is one option among many. They look further apart on numbers than they are, because they measure different things. Pass 1 measures the share of one outlet's peak orders (15% by week 4). Pass 2 measures the share of a whole campus's students ordering weekly (3 to 6% after three months). On a 3,000-student campus, 90 to 180 weekly orderers placing 1.5 to 2 orders a week is 135 to 360 pre-orders a week. If the busiest counter serves about 500 to 600 orders a week in its peak windows (an assumption), that is roughly 20 to 50% of its peak orders, above Pass 1's bar. So Pass 2's base expectation is reachable on a campus with one busy counter and a real queue, and fails on a campus without. The genuinely new point from Pass 2 is price: Swiggy's Toing has taught students to expect app prices at or below the counter, so Juicy must never be dearer than walking up, including any tax. **What settles it:** the single-outlet pilot, at the real price, with both metrics tracked.

**Competitors.** Pass 1 mapped the business-to-business field in depth (who holds which campus, what the counter software costs, who has traction). Pass 2 mapped the consumer field (Toing, Deskeats, Ownly's newer numbers, Flipkart, ONDC's decline) and confirmed the US exit precedent. They do not contradict each other. There is one tension inside the evidence: CLSA's estimate puts Toing's weekly users near Zomato's, while Eternal calls its impact "limited". Both can be true: Toing can be on many phones while barely denting Zomato's orders (Eternal still grew 20% in the quarter). For Juicy the relevant fact is simpler: in Bengaluru, students already have a no-fee app with counter-matching prices.

**Market size.** This is the largest apparent gap, and it is an illusion of scope. On campuses alone the two passes almost agree: about ₹162 to 170 crore a year of platform revenue for India at 10%, and ₹4 to 6 crore for Bengaluru. Pass 1's larger headline (up to ₹1,200 crore for India, ₹46 crore base for Bengaluru) comes from adding offices, branded cafés and quick-service counters. Pass 2 treats offices as closed, because Swiggy's Deskeats is in 7,000 tech parks and corporate cafeterias are contracted to HungerBox, SmartQ and Sodexo; both passes agree branded cafés already have their own apps. **What to believe:** plan on the campus-only figure, and at the price outlets will actually pay (2 to 3%, or a flat fee) that is ₹35 to 50 crore across India and ₹1 to 2 crore in Bengaluru. Treat offices and cafés as later, contested upside.

**Unit economics.** Both agree on the shape: the per-order money is positive at 10%, thin at anything lower while Juicy handles the money, and the fixed costs need a lot of orders. Pass 2's break-even of about 18,900 orders a month equals roughly 38 outlets at Pass 1's base volume (500 orders each) or 19 at its aggressive volume (1,000 each). Pass 1's base model reaches 30,000 orders a month by month 24 and still loses money, because the team grows with it. Same story. Pass 2's structural points (fixed customer fees are how the aggregators solved small tickets; the GST wedge; the escrow gate) strengthen Pass 1's advice to pilot with the outlet collecting its own money.

**Differentiation.** Pass 1 says "inside a captive canteen Juicy is not another food app"; Pass 2 says "it is another food app". They are describing two different businesses. As designed in the roadmap (a commission marketplace for many restaurants with discovery by area), it is another food app, and both passes say so. Repositioned as the canteen's own ordering and counter-operations system, sold as software to the outlet or the institution, it is not. That is a change of business model, not a rewrite of the code. Both also agree the surplus-deals feature should wait; Pass 2's sharper point is that reselling uncollected prepaid food only earns money when no-shows are frequent, that is, when the core experience is failing.

| ---------------------------------------- | ---------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | --------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- | -------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------------- |

Petpooja's FY25 revenue of ₹101 crore over about 1 lakh restaurants is about ₹10,000 per restaurant per year; at ₹12,000 a month it would be over ₹1,400 crore | About ₹12,000 a year. | Hangry                                   | $125 million of campus transactions in the year before its sale                                                  | About $200 million processed                                                                                                                             | Different measures (one year versus cumulative)                                                                                                                                                                                                     | Not a conflict                                                                                                                                                                                     |
