| GST on orders from unregistered canteens | Juicy is almost certainly an e-commerce operator; whether a pickup-only platform owes the 5% needs a tax adviser | Its recollection of the 2021 CBIC circular is that the operator is liable even when the canteen is unregistered; its secondary source reads the opposite | The circular could not be fetched by either pass                                                                                                                                                                                                    | Unresolved. Get a chartered accountant's written answer in week 1 (section 18, experiment 9)                                                                                                       |

### D.4 What each pass found that the other missed

**Found only by Pass 2:** Toing's scale and its student targeting; Swiggy Deskeats in 7,000 tech parks; Flipkart's Bengaluru test; Ownly's latest numbers; ONDC food's decline; the $300 million DoorDash deal; Razorpay Route's ₹40 lakh turnover gate; FSSAI's July 2026 order against Toing's licence; the GST wedge; "restaurants stay where the customers are"; NRAI's warning of negative gross margins during the 2026 LPG crisis; Swiggy's 2.5% direct-ordering pilot of 2021; and the AISHE size split (only 18.2% of colleges have more than 1,000 students, so about 9,300 campuses are big enough to have a queue).

**Found only by Pass 1:** the Delhi college canteen tender (committee-fixed prices, the licence fee, fines for changing prices); SpaceBasic and GoKhana; HungerBox's ₹63 average order and ₹5.9 of revenue per order; Thrive's tiny lifetime order value per restaurant; Petpooja's revenue and loss; SNACC's shutdown over small baskets; the 2022 Dineout walkout; restaurant software raising $0.5 million in 2026; the finding that nobody has ever measured an Indian canteen queue; app-retention data; Swiggy's ₹36.5 of contribution per order; and the idea of piloting with the outlet collecting its own money.

### D.5 What to believe overall

**Highest confidence (both passes, independently):**

1. The queue problem is real but small; order-ahead works only where the outlet or institution makes it the default.
2. The 10% commission is the weakest part of the plan. A flat ₹1,000 to 3,000 a month, or 0 to 3% per order, is what outlets will bear.
3. HungerBox, SmartQ and MealPe-type players already sell this to institutions; the open niche is small, uncontracted canteens.
4. The campus-only market is about ₹160 to 170 crore a year for India at 10%, and a few crore for Bengaluru.
5. At ₹200 tickets the gateway eats about a quarter of a 10% commission, and a two-to-three-person team needs roughly 19,000 orders a month to break even.
6. The defensible version is canteen software or an institutional rail, not a consumer marketplace.
7. The likely good outcome is a small, profitable, campus-by-campus business; venture scale needs institution contracts and the campus payment layer.

**Moderate confidence (one pass found it, nothing in the other contradicts it):** students are now price-trained by Toing; a 5% GST line could make Juicy dearer than the counter; the escrow product may not be available to a pre-revenue startup; offices are largely closed to a newcomer.

**Unresolved, to settle in the next 30 days:** how long the queue really is at your target counters; how many orders one outlet actually moves to pre-order; what an owner actually pays when asked; whether Juicy owes 5% GST as a pickup-only platform; whether a split-settlement product is open to you; and whether Zomato or Swiggy still offer pickup on your campus.

## Part E: The next 30 days (a validation roadmap)

The goal of the month is not to build. It is to find out, with numbers you can show someone, whether one outlet and its customers want this badly enough to use it every day and to pay for it. Code only what the test needs.

### Week 1: measure the pain

Objective: know whether the queue problem is real and who owns the decision.

- Run the queue study at 3 to 5 outlets (one college canteen, one college café, one tea or snack shop at a college gate, one standalone café near a campus). Record wait times, line lengths and walk-aways at the peak.
- Do 10 owner/manager interviews and 20 customer interviews with the question lists in this report. Do not mention Juicy.
- Put up the landing page and share it in one campus's WhatsApp groups and Instagram.
- Send the payments and GST questions in writing (section 18, experiment 9), and open Zomato and Swiggy at the target campus to see whether a pickup or takeaway option appears for its counters.
- Fix the one gap in the current app that every pilot needs: a "pause orders" switch and a chosen pickup time. Nothing else.

Metrics: average peak wait; share of orders in the busiest hour; how many operators name peak chaos unprompted; waitlist signups.

Decision point (end of week 1): If peak waits are under 3 minutes at most outlets and fewer than 1 in 3 operators mention it, stop and rethink the problem (the product may still fit canteens that run on prepaid coupons or office cafeterias with fixed lunch slots; find out which pain is real). Otherwise continue.

### Week 2: run it by hand

Objective: see the behaviour before writing more software.

- Pick the outlet with the worst measured queue and a friendly owner. Start the WhatsApp concierge: students text their order, you type it into the staff board, they pay at the counter.
- Put the fake-door poster up at two other outlets.
- Finish the interviews (15 operators, 30 customers, 8 staff). Ask the pricing questions.
- Approach 15 outlets with the one-page pilot offer: free for a month, with the real price options shown on day 15 (section 18, experiment 7).

Metrics: WhatsApp orders per day and share of peak orders; repeat users; no-shows; poster scans; pilot yeses with the fee.

Decision point: fewer than 3% of the outlet's peak orders by WhatsApp by day 14, or zero outlets willing to pay even a token fee, means the pain is not strong enough for this product in this form. A yes from 4 or more outlets and 10% of peak orders is a green light.

### Week 3: run it with the real app

Objective: find out whether the software keeps the promise under a real rush.

- Switch the concierge outlet to the real app: QR on the counter and on tables, pay at the counter or by the outlet's own UPI QR, a tablet for the staff board, and a manual "ready" WhatsApp message (or web push if it is ready). Charge exactly the counter price; if the accountant says a GST line is unavoidable, show it, so the test measures the real price gap.
- Recruit 3 student ambassadors; first order gets a small free item, paid for by you.
- Keep a support log: every complaint, every mistake, every minute the board was ignored.

Metrics: share of peak orders; day-7 retention; promised-time accuracy; order mistakes; the share who choose to prepay; staff tolerance.

Decision point: the app must handle the rush with 97% of orders ready on time and no more than 1 support message per 20 orders. If staff abandon the board during the rush, the product is not usable yet, regardless of demand.

### Week 4: ask for money and decide

Objective: turn observations into a go/no-go with evidence.

- Ask the pilot outlet to pay for next month. Record the answer and the reason.
- Pull the week-4 numbers: weekly active customers, repeat rate, orders per active user, no-shows.
- Write the one-page pilot report with the numbers against the thresholds set in week 1.

Decision point (end of the month):

- Go: 15% or more of peak orders, 30% or more week-1 users still active, at least 3% of the campus's students ordering weekly without a discount, the outlet pays or commits in writing, and 3 or more other outlets want in. Next step is the single-campus launch (3 to 5 outlets, 8 weeks), with the payment paperwork started now, because it takes weeks and experiment 9 will have shown which route is open.
- Change: strong demand but nobody will pay, or outlets love it but students do not return. Change the model (for example raise the outlet's flat fee, or sell a campus-wide package to the college; never charge students more) and re-run week 3.
- Stop: under 5% of peak orders and under 10% retention after a real pilot at the worst-queue outlet you could find. The problem is not painful enough here; keep the code, change the problem.
